*   **Language Diversity:** Exploring the impact of different prompt languages is a unique and valuable contribution.
*   **Reasoning Analysis:** the internal processes within the DeepSeek model shed some light on the decision-making processes within the models.
*   **Statistical Quantification:** The use of statistical tests (x2, Cramér's V) and the introduced "randomness index" offers a quantitative measure of randomness and bias.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-organized, clearly presents its findings, and provides insightful examples.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Task Complexity:** The task of generating a single random number is simplistic and may not fully reflect the nuances of randomness in more complex applications of LLMs.
*   **Generalization:** The specific biases observed might be highly dependent on the training data of the specific models used. It is unclear how readily these findings would generalize to future, differently trained LLMs.
*   **Lack of Mitigation Strategies:** While the paper identifies biases, it offers limited discussion or exploration of strategies to mitigate these issues and improve the randomness of LLM-generated outputs.
*   **Python "Random" comparison**: Assumed T=1.0 value when not appropriate

**Influence:**

The paper could influence future research in several areas:

*   **Bias Mitigation:** It might stimulate the development of techniques to reduce biases in LLM training data and sampling procedures.
*   **Trustworthy AI:**  The findings contribute to the broader discussion on the trustworthiness and reliability of AI systems, particularly in critical applications.
*   **LLM Evaluation:** The defined randomness index is a quick and easy metric to add to existing model evaluation methods.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **6.5**. This score reflects the paper's valuable contribution to the understanding of randomness generation in LLMs. The paper is a well-executed systematic exploration that introduces a new perspective on the inherent limitations of LLMs, while simultaneously providing insights into our own cognitive biases as reflected in the training data. The inclusion of cross-linguistic assessment has strong value in this project. The paper demonstrates the somewhat deterministic nature of LLM 'random' output which is an observation with significance for various applications. While limited in scope and not groundbreaking in its findings, the study is a solid piece of empirical work that contributes incrementally to the field, and its inclusion in the scope will provide additional value to this approach. The study would also benefit by discussing possible methods for mitigating this bias. The score takes into consideration the limitations in task complexity and the lack of concrete mitigation strategies, which would need to be more extensively addressed to warrant a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 0/10

### Can Large Language Models Unveil the Mysteries? An Exploration of Their Ability to Unlock Information in Complex Scenarios
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19973v1)
- **Authors**: Chao Wang, Luning Zhang, Zheng Wang, Yang Zhou
- **Abstract**: Combining multiple perceptual inputs and performing combinatorial reasoning in complex scenarios is a sophisticated cognitive function in humans. With advancements in multi-modal large language models, recent benchmarks tend to evaluate visual understanding across multiple images. However, they often overlook the necessity of combinatorial reasoning across multiple perceptual information. To explore the ability of advanced models to integrate multiple perceptual inputs for combinatorial reasoning in complex scenarios, we introduce two benchmarks: Clue-Visual Question Answering (CVQA), with three task types to assess visual comprehension and synthesis, and Clue of Password-Visual Question Answering (CPVQA), with two task types focused on accurate interpretation and application of visual data. For our benchmarks, we present three plug-and-play approaches: utilizing model input for reasoning, enhancing reasoning through minimum margin decoding with randomness generation, and retrieving semantically relevant visual information for effective data integration. The combined results reveal current models' poor performance on combinatorial reasoning benchmarks, even the state-of-the-art (SOTA) closed-source model achieves only 33.04% accuracy on CVQA, and drops to 7.38% on CPVQA. Notably, our approach improves the performance of models on combinatorial reasoning, with a 22.17% boost on CVQA and 9.40% on CPVQA over the SOTA closed-source model, demonstrating its effectiveness in enhancing combinatorial reasoning with multiple perceptual inputs in complex scenarios. The code will be publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces two new benchmarks, Clue-Visual Question Answering (CVQA) and Clue of Password-Visual Question Answering (CPVQA), designed to evaluate the ability of multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) to perform combinatorial reasoning across multiple perceptual inputs in complex scenarios.  The authors note existing benchmarks tend to focus on individual images and often single facts. They present three "plug-and-play" methods to enhance model performance without extensive prompt engineering: using model input for reasoning, enhancing reasoning through minimum margin decoding with randomness generation, and retrieving semantically relevant visual information.  Results show current models, even SOTA closed-source models, perform poorly on these benchmarks.  The authors' methods improve performance, but accuracy remains low.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main novelty lies in the *specific focus* on combinatorial reasoning across *multiple perceptual inputs* within complex, visually-grounded scenarios. While other datasets exist that test visual reasoning or incorporate multiple images, the explicit design to force combinatorial thinking about the relationships *between* multiple visual elements is a more refined contribution. This represents a move away from datasets primarily focused on single-image understanding, a trend this paper identifies and tries to address. Furthermore, while the individual components of their three plug-and-play methods are not revolutionary, the combination and application in this specific combinatorial context are a reasonable novel aspect. The benchmark's consistent thematic selection of visually similar images from the same game level further contributes to its novel construction and provides a unique consistency.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper hinges on whether combinatorial reasoning across multiple perceptual inputs is, in fact, a *bottleneck* for current MLLMs. If so, the CVQA and CPVQA benchmarks provide a useful tool to measure progress and inform future model development. The low baseline performance of existing models strongly suggests there *is* a genuine gap in capabilities. The proposed "plug-and-play" methods, while not achieving stellar results, point towards potentially fruitful avenues for improvement: a) enriching inputs with LLM-generated image captions; b) using uncertainty measures to guide reasoning and randomness; and c) retrieval to focus on visually relevant information. The demonstration that simple techniques can yield significant gains further indicates that MLLM capabilities in this area are underdeveloped. One weakness is the reliance on a single game as the source of images. While visually consistent, this limits the generalizability of the benchmarks. A broader range of scenarios would increase the benchmark's impact and relevance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing benchmarks and the need for targeted evaluation of combinatorial reasoning.
    *   **Well-Defined Benchmarks:** CVQA and CPVQA are thoughtfully designed to test specific aspects of combinatorial reasoning.
    *   **Plug-and-Play Methods:** The proposed methods are relatively simple to implement and provide a starting point for further research.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough evaluation of various models, providing valuable insights into their strengths and weaknesses.
    * The statistical validity of the benchmark expansion is well demonstrated through the inclusion of figures comparing original and extended datasets.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Image Source:** Relying solely on images from a single game ('Can You Escape?') limits the generalizability of the benchmarks.
    *   **Modest Performance Gains:** While the proposed methods improve performance, the overall accuracy remains low, indicating that significant challenges remain.
    *   **Lack of theoretical grounding:** While the three plug-and-play methods presented improve model performance, there lacks strong theoretical evidence provided to ground these advancements beyond observations within the benchmarks provided.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's influence will depend on whether the community adopts and builds upon the CVQA and CPVQA benchmarks. The paper also inspires more research and interest in using more than one image as input, since currently most MLLMs can only process a single image. If these benchmarks become standard evaluation tools, the paper could have a significant impact on the direction of MLLM research, shifting the focus towards more complex reasoning tasks. This might inspire work to more easily allow MLLMs to easily use multiple visual inputs in addition to language.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper represents a solid contribution but has limitations. The problem definition and benchmark design are strong. The proposed methods are adequate for proof-of-concept, but are not transformative on their own. The low baseline performance is also a strong indicator that the research tackles an important current deficiency of SOTA MLLMs. However, the reliance on a single game and the relatively modest performance gains achieved by the proposed methods limit the overall impact. Also, the paper could benefit from more theoretical grounding beyond just observed results within the benchmarks, as previously mentioned.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### The Lookahead Limitation: Why Multi-Operand Addition is Hard for LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19981v1)
- **Authors**: Tanja Baeumel, Josef van Genabith, Simon Ostermann
- **Abstract**: Autoregressive large language models (LLMs) exhibit impressive performance across various tasks but struggle with simple arithmetic, such as addition of two or more operands. We show that this struggle arises from LLMs' use of a simple one-digit lookahead heuristic, which works fairly well (but not perfect) for two-operand addition but fails in multi-operand cases, where the carry-over logic is more complex. Our probing experiments and digit-wise accuracy evaluation show that LLMs fail precisely where a one-digit lookahead is insufficient to account for cascading carries. We analyze the impact of tokenization strategies on arithmetic performance and show that all investigated models, regardless of tokenization, are inherently limited in the addition of multiple operands due to their reliance on a one-digit lookahead heuristic. Our findings reveal fundamental limitations that prevent LLMs from generalizing to more complex numerical reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary of the paper and a critical evaluation:

**Summary**

The paper investigates why Large Language Models (LLMs) struggle with multi-operand addition, a seemingly simple arithmetic task. The authors hypothesize that LLMs rely on a one-digit lookahead heuristic when performing addition, which is effective for two-operand addition but fails in the more complex carry-over scenarios of multi-operand addition. Through probing experiments and digit-wise accuracy evaluations, the paper presents evidence that LLMs indeed fail precisely in cases where a one-digit lookahead is insufficient to account for cascading carries. They also show that this limitation holds regardless of the tokenization strategy used by the LLM. The paper concludes that this reliance on a simple heuristic explains the lack of robustness in LLMs' arithmetic performance and reveals a fundamental limitation preventing them from generalizing to more complex numerical reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance**

The paper offers a valuable contribution to understanding the limitations of LLMs, moving beyond simply documenting the failure modes to proposing and testing a plausible explanation. The strength of the paper lies in its methodical approach, combining hypothesis formulation, targeted experimentation, and analysis of results to support its central argument. The paper doesn’t simply identify that LLMs struggle with arithmetic, a well-documented problem; instead, it aims to pinpoint a _specific reason_ why they struggle by linking it to the inherent architecture of LLMs and their inability to easily perform recursive or procedural tasks requiring explicit carry operations.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Hypothesis:** The one-digit lookahead heuristic is clearly articulated and provides a testable explanation for LLM arithmetic performance.
    *   **Methodological Rigor:** The use of probing experiments and digit-wise accuracy evaluations provides strong empirical support for the hypothesis. The controlled datasets are well-designed to isolate specific carry scenarios. The analyses of multiple models with differing tokenization schemes strengthens the generalizability of their findings. The comparison between the experimental performance and the predicted accuracy derived from the heuristic is convincing.
    *   **Addresses a Core Issue:** The paper tackles a fundamental question about LLM limitations: why do they struggle with tasks requiring a shift from left-to-right processing to right-to-left computations needed for addition?
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper investigates multiple tokenization strategies.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Arithmetic Operations:** The paper focuses solely on addition, leaving open the question of whether the one-digit lookahead limitation extends to other arithmetic operations like subtraction, multiplication, or division. However, the authors explicitly acknowledge this limitation.
    *   **Doesn't Offer Solutions:** While the paper identifies a limitation, it does not propose or evaluate methods for overcoming this lookahead limitation. However, the authors suggest that targeted training to improve the lookahead capabilities of LLMs.
    *   **Oversimplification Potential:** While the lookahead heuristic is insightful, it could be an oversimplification of the complex processes happening inside an LLM. There might be other contributing factors that the paper doesn't fully capture. The heuristic could also be considered a post-hoc explanation of an observed failure, rather than a demonstrated causal driver of the failure.
    *   **Model Scaling Question:** While the paper investigates different models, it doesn't address the question of whether scaling models *within the same family* impacts this lookahead limitation. It is possible that the one-digit lookahead capability emerges as a property of the model architecture and training procedure, but then could extend to more steps in larger models.
    *   **Training Data Sensitivity:** The paper mentions limited exposure to many-operand addition tasks as a possible factor, but it would be worth exploring if increasing the training data of these cases could improve the LLMs’ capabilities.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper goes beyond simply observing LLMs' difficulties with arithmetic and offers a clear, testable hypothesis. The "one digit lookahead" heuristic is a new and insightful way of conceptualizing the issue.
    *   The findings have implications for understanding the broader limitations of LLMs in tasks requiring numerical reasoning.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-supported, insightful explanation for LLMs' difficulties with arithmetic. While its scope is limited to addition and doesn't offer solutions, the rigorous methodology, insightful hypothesis, and comprehensive analysis justify a high score. It makes a significant contribution to our understanding of why LLMs struggle with this kind of task.

### Erasing Without Remembering: Safeguarding Knowledge Forgetting in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19982v1)
- **Authors**: Huazheng Wang, Yongcheng Jing, Haifeng Sun, Yingjie Wang, Jingyu Wang, Jianxin Liao, Dacheng Tao
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we explore machine unlearning from a novel dimension, by studying how to safeguard model unlearning in large language models (LLMs). Our goal is to prevent unlearned models from recalling any related memory of the targeted knowledge.We begin by uncovering a surprisingly simple yet overlooked fact: existing methods typically erase only the exact expressions of the targeted knowledge, leaving paraphrased or related information intact. To rigorously measure such oversights, we introduce UGBench, the first benchmark tailored for evaluating the generalisation performance across 13 state-of-the-art methods.UGBench reveals that unlearned models can still recall paraphrased answers and retain target facts in intermediate layers. To address this, we propose PERMU, a perturbation-based method that significantly enhances the generalisation capabilities for safeguarding LLM unlearning.Experiments demonstrate that PERMU delivers up to a 50.13% improvement in unlearning while maintaining a 43.53% boost in robust generalisation. Our code can be found in https://github.com/MaybeLizzy/UGBench.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Erasing Without Remembering: Safeguarding Knowledge Forgetting in Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of incomplete knowledge unlearning in large language models (LLMs). It highlights that existing unlearning methods primarily focus on erasing the *exact expressions* of targeted knowledge, leaving paraphrased or related information intact. To rigorously evaluate this issue, the authors introduce UGBENCH, a new benchmark designed to assess the generalization capabilities of unlearning methods. UGBENCH reveals that unlearned models often retain paraphrased answers and knowledge within intermediate layers. To combat this, the authors propose PERMU, a perturbation-based method that introduces noise into the embeddings of subject tokens in unlearning samples to disrupt factual recall and improve generalization. Experimental results demonstrate that PERMU significantly enhances unlearning performance and robust generalization while maintaining model utility.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in identifying and formalizing the problem of *incomplete unlearning* in LLMs, specifically the failure of existing methods to generalize to paraphrased or related knowledge. The introduction of UGBENCH as a means of evaluating this generalization is a significant contribution. The PERMU method, while leveraging adversarial examples, presents a practical approach tailored for addressing the identified generalization shortcomings in LLM unlearning. However, perturbation-based approaches are not entirely novel, and the degree of novelty of the PERMU method itself is moderate.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to improve the reliability and trustworthiness of LLMs. By highlighting the limitations of existing unlearning techniques and proposing a more robust solution, the paper contributes to safer and more responsible AI development. Incomplete unlearning poses risks related to privacy violations and the persistence of harmful information. PERMU addresses these risks and is crucial for responsible AI practices. The work's practical implications, demonstrated through improvements in unlearning and generalization, suggest a potentially high impact on the field.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Rigorous evaluation using the proposed UGBENCH benchmark.
    *   Empirical evidence demonstrating the limitations of existing methods.
    *   A practical and effective solution (PERMU) for improving unlearning generalization.
    *   Comprehensive experiments and ablation studies.
    *   Code availability for reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The PERMU method, while effective, relies on a relatively simple perturbation strategy and might not be sufficient for more sophisticated attacks or knowledge representations.
    *   The paper primarily focuses on factual knowledge unlearning and might not be directly applicable to unlearning other types of information (e.g., stylistic biases or harmful associations).
    *   The computational cost of PERMU compared to simpler methods is not thoroughly discussed.
    *   The selection of datasets, although comprehensive, could benefit from human evaluation to confirm if identified relations are "in-scope"

*   **Potential Influence:**
    The paper is likely to influence future research in LLM unlearning by:
    *   Shifting the focus towards generalization as a critical evaluation metric.
    *   Providing a useful benchmark (UGBENCH) for assessing unlearning performance.
    *   Inspiring the development of new and more robust unlearning techniques.
    *   Promoting the adoption of safer and more responsible AI practices.
    *   Serving as a foundation for incorporating human evaluations in assessment.

*   **Justification for Score:**
    The paper makes a valuable contribution by identifying and addressing an important limitation of existing LLM unlearning methods. The introduction of UGBENCH, a new generalisation evaluation benchmark, and the PERMU method showcase clear advancements in the state-of-the-art. The limitations of the PERMU method, as detailed in the weaknesses, along with opportunities for further research, prevent the paper from being considered a perfect score.

### 3D-AffordanceLLM: Harnessing Large Language Models for Open-Vocabulary Affordance Detection in 3D Worlds
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20041v1)
- **Authors**: Hengshuo Chu, Xiang Deng, Xiaoyang Chen, Yinchuan Li, Jianye Hao, Liqiang Nie
- **Abstract**: 3D Affordance detection is a challenging problem with broad applications on various robotic tasks. Existing methods typically formulate the detection paradigm as a label-based semantic segmentation task. This paradigm relies on predefined labels and lacks the ability to comprehend complex natural language, resulting in limited generalization in open-world scene. To address these limitations, we reformulate the traditional affordance detection paradigm into \textit{Instruction Reasoning Affordance Segmentation} (IRAS) task. This task is designed to output a affordance mask region given a query reasoning text, which avoids fixed categories of input labels. We accordingly propose the \textit{3D-AffordanceLLM} (3D-ADLLM), a framework designed for reasoning affordance detection in 3D open-scene. Specifically, 3D-ADLLM introduces large language models (LLMs) to 3D affordance perception with a custom-designed decoder for generating affordance masks, thus achieving open-world reasoning affordance detection. In addition, given the scarcity of 3D affordance datasets for training large models, we seek to extract knowledge from general segmentation data and transfer it to affordance detection. Thus, we propose a multi-stage training strategy that begins with a novel pre-training task, i.e., \textit{Referring Object Part Segmentation}~(ROPS). This stage is designed to equip the model with general recognition and segmentation capabilities at the object-part level. Then followed by fine-tuning with the IRAS task, 3D-ADLLM obtains the reasoning ability for affordance detection. In summary, 3D-ADLLM leverages the rich world knowledge and human-object interaction reasoning ability of LLMs, achieving approximately an 8\% improvement in mIoU on open-vocabulary affordance detection tasks.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper, including a novelty/significance score with detailed justification.

**Summary**

The paper introduces 3D-AffordanceLLM (3D-ADLLM), a framework designed for open-vocabulary affordance detection in 3D scenes. Addressing the limitations of traditional affordance detection methods that rely on predefined labels, the authors reformulate the problem as an Instruction Reasoning Affordance Segmentation (IRAS) task.  3D-ADLLM integrates large language models (LLMs) to leverage world knowledge and reasoning capabilities for affordance detection, using a custom-designed decoder to generate affordance masks. To overcome the scarcity of 3D affordance data, the authors propose a multi-stage training strategy: a pre-training task, Referring Object Part Segmentation (ROPS), to equip the model with general recognition and segmentation abilities at the object-part level, followed by fine-tuning on the IRAS task.  The paper reports an approximately 8% improvement in mIoU on open-vocabulary affordance detection tasks.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the integration of LLMs for 3D affordance detection within an open-vocabulary setting. While existing methods have started incorporating text encoders, this paper pushes the boundaries by leveraging the reasoning and world knowledge inherent in LLMs. The reformulation of the task into IRAS is also a significant shift away from label-based paradigms. The multi-stage training approach with the novel ROPS pre-training task addresses the data scarcity issue in 3D affordance learning.

*   **Significance:** Affordance detection is a critical component of robotic perception and manipulation. The ability to understand natural language instructions and reason about affordances in open-world scenarios is a major step towards more adaptable and intelligent robots. By enabling zero-shot learning and flexible reasoning through LLMs, the paper addresses significant limitations of previous methods. The performance gains, though relatively moderate (8% mIoU improvement), are promising, particularly considering the complexity of the task and the challenging open-vocabulary setting.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Formulation:** The IRAS task effectively reframes the problem, enabling more flexible interaction and overcoming the limitations of predefined label sets.
    *   **LLM Integration:**  Successfully incorporates LLMs, leveraging their reasoning and world knowledge for 3D affordance perception. The design of the Affordance Decoder and the special token `<AFF>` is a clever method for translating LLM understanding into segmentation masks.
    *   **Multi-Stage Training:** The ROPS pre-training task is a practical and effective solution to mitigate the problem of limited 3D affordance data.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** Provides comprehensive experimental results comparing to existing methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Moderate Performance Gains:**  The 8% mIoU improvement, while notable, is not a monumental leap. While the paper addresses significant limitations, there is room for substantial improvement.
    *   **Reliance on Existing LLMs:** The reliance on pre-trained LLMs means the performance is capped by the capabilities of the chosen LLM (Phi-3.5 and Qwen).
    *   **Complexity:** The overall framework, with its multi-stage training and various components, is relatively complex. This can make it difficult to reproduce and extend.
    *   **Limited OOD Generalization:** While the paper presents promising OOD results, the results are not very high overall, suggesting that the gains of introducing the LLM are not fully reflected in the ability to generalize to new unseen data.
    *   **Limited ablations:** The effect of using multi-stage training could be better quantified.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence the field in several ways:

    *   **Shift in Paradigm:** The IRAS task and the use of LLMs can inspire a shift in the way affordance detection is approached, moving towards more flexible and reasoning-based methods.
    *   **New Research Directions:** The framework opens up new research directions, such as exploring different LLM architectures, developing more sophisticated affordance decoders, and designing more effective pre-training tasks.
    *   **Robotics Applications:** The advancements in affordance detection can have a direct impact on various robotic applications, enabling more intelligent and adaptable robots.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to a fundamental problem in robotics, leveraging LLMs to address limitations in traditional affordance detection. The task reformulation and multi-stage training strategy are key strengths. However, the moderate performance gains and complexity of the framework, along with its reliance on existing LLMs, limit its overall significance. Therefore, I assign a score that reflects the promising nature of the work while acknowledging its shortcomings.

### Polish-ASTE: Aspect-Sentiment Triplet Extraction Datasets for Polish
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20046v1)
- **Authors**: Marta Lango, Borys Naglik, Mateusz Lango, Iwo Naglik
- **Abstract**: Aspect-Sentiment Triplet Extraction (ASTE) is one of the most challenging and complex tasks in sentiment analysis. It concerns the construction of triplets that contain an aspect, its associated sentiment polarity, and an opinion phrase that serves as a rationale for the assigned polarity. Despite the growing popularity of the task and the many machine learning methods being proposed to address it, the number of datasets for ASTE is very limited. In particular, no dataset is available for any of the Slavic languages. In this paper, we present two new datasets for ASTE containing customer opinions about hotels and purchased products expressed in Polish. We also perform experiments with two ASTE techniques combined with two large language models for Polish to investigate their performance and the difficulty of the assembled datasets. The new datasets are available under a permissive licence and have the same file format as the English datasets, facilitating their use in future research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces two new datasets for Aspect-Sentiment Triplet Extraction (ASTE) in Polish, a task that involves extracting aspect phrases, sentiment polarity, and opinion phrases from text. These datasets contain customer reviews of hotels and purchased products from the Wroclaw Corpus of Consumer Reviews Sentiment (WCCRS). The authors annotate these datasets with ASTE triples and compare their characteristics to existing English ASTE datasets. They also conduct experiments using two ASTE techniques combined with Polish language models (HerBERT and TrelBERT) to assess the difficulty of the new datasets and evaluate existing ASTE methods on Polish.  The datasets are released under a permissive license with the same file format as existing English datasets to facilitate their use.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The primary novelty lies in the creation and release of the first ASTE datasets for the Polish language. While the general task of ASTE is well-established, the lack of resources for languages other than English is a significant gap in the field.  The paper addresses this gap directly.  However, the methodology for dataset creation – annotation of an existing corpus – is relatively standard.  The comparison with existing English datasets provides valuable context but doesn't represent a substantial methodological innovation.

* **Significance:** The significance stems from providing a much-needed resource for ASTE research in Polish. This allows researchers to:
    * Adapt and evaluate existing ASTE models on a new language.
    * Develop new ASTE models specifically tailored to the characteristics of Polish (e.g., its morphology).
    * Conduct cross-lingual comparisons of sentiment analysis techniques.

The experiments conducted, while basic, provide a useful baseline for future research. The analysis of dataset characteristics highlights the differences between Polish and English datasets, suggesting that language-specific adaptations are likely necessary. The authors clearly state their goal to incentivize comparison of ASTE models on the benchmark by providing open source datasets which is an important consideration.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Addresses a gap:** Fills a crucial void in ASTE research by providing datasets for a Slavic language.
    * **Open and accessible:**  The permissive license and standard file format increase usability and promote wider adoption.
    * **Dataset analysis:** The analysis of dataset characteristics is insightful and helps understand the challenges of ASTE in Polish.
    * **Baseline experiments:** Provides a basic performance evaluation using established models, offering a starting point for future research.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Annotation Methodology:** Although the dataset is novel, the annotation is based on an existing corpus and follows well-established guidelines.  More details regarding inter-annotator reliability (beyond the 78% agreement) would strengthen the work. The exact annotation guidelines could benefit from more detail, although a summary is provided.
    * **Limited Experiments:**  The experiments are relatively simple and only involve adapting existing models. A more in-depth exploration of Polish-specific features and their impact on ASTE performance would be valuable.
    * **Limited novelty in technical contribution:** The experiments do not propose novel methods or architectures specifically designed to handle the Polish language, using established techniques.

* **Potential Influence:** The datasets have the potential to become a valuable resource for the sentiment analysis community, particularly for researchers working on ASTE in Polish or cross-lingual sentiment analysis. They will enable more rigorous and relevant evaluation of ASTE techniques.

**Overall Rationale:**

The paper's primary contribution is the creation and open release of the first Polish ASTE datasets. This fulfills a clear need in the research community and facilitates future research in ASTE for low-resource languages. While the annotation and experimental methodologies are relatively standard, the value of the datasets and their potential impact on the field are undeniable. However, more detail on annotation, inter-annotator agreement, and consideration on Polish language models would have improved the paper.

### Collab-Overcooked: Benchmarking and Evaluating Large Language Models as Collaborative Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20073v1)
- **Authors**: Haochen Sun, Shuwen Zhang, Lei Ren, Hao Xu, Hao Fu, Caixia Yuan, Xiaojie Wang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) based agent systems have made great strides in real-world applications beyond traditional NLP tasks. This paper proposes a new LLM-powered Multi-Agent System (LLM-MAS) benchmark, Collab-Overcooked, built on the popular Overcooked-AI game with more applicable and challenging tasks in interactive environments. Collab-Overcooked extends existing benchmarks from two novel perspectives. First, it provides a multi-agent framework supporting diverse tasks and objectives and encourages collaboration through natural language communication. Second, it introduces a spectrum of process-oriented evaluation metrics to assess the fine-grained collaboration capabilities of different LLM agents, a dimension often overlooked in prior work. We conduct extensive experiments over 10 popular LLMs and show that, while the LLMs present a strong ability in goal interpretation, there is a significant discrepancy in active collaboration and continuous adaption that are critical for efficiently fulfilling complicated tasks. Notably, we highlight the strengths and weaknesses in LLM-MAS and provide insights for improving and evaluating LLM-MAS on a unified and open-sourced benchmark. Environments, 30 open-ended tasks, and an integrated evaluation package are now publicly available at https://github.com/YusaeMeow/Collab-Overcooked.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper "Collab-Overcooked: Benchmarking and Evaluating Large Language Models as Collaborative Agents". Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Collab-Overcooked, a new benchmark for evaluating the collaborative abilities of Large Language Model-based Multi-Agent Systems (LLM-MAS). Built upon the popular Overcooked-AI game, Collab-Overcooked features more complex and realistic tasks that require agents to actively collaborate through natural language communication.  The benchmark introduces a framework supporting diverse tasks and process-oriented evaluation metrics (Trajectory Efficiency Score - TES and Incremental Trajectory Efficiency Score - ITES) for fine-grained assessment of collaboration capabilities beyond just task completion rates.  The authors conduct experiments on 10 popular LLMs, highlighting strengths and weaknesses in goal interpretation, active collaboration, and dynamic adaptation. They open-source the environment, tasks, and evaluation package.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addressing a Gap:** The paper clearly identifies a crucial gap in existing LLM-MAS benchmarks: the lack of rigorous evaluation of *collaboration itself*, focusing too much on task completion without ensuring genuine cooperation.
    *   **Process-Oriented Metrics:**  The introduction of TES and ITES is a positive step. These metrics attempt to move beyond simple success/failure and capture *how well* agents are collaborating during the process.
    *   **Resource Isolation and Asymmetric Knowledge:** The design choices in Collab-Overcooked – resource isolation and asymmetric task knowledge– actively *force* collaboration, preventing agents from succeeding independently.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors evaluate a wide range of LLMs, including both open-source and closed-source models, offering a valuable comparative analysis.
    *   **Open-Source Contribution:** The open-sourcing of the benchmark is a significant contribution, enabling future research and development in the field.
    *   **Failure Analysis:** The "Failure Analysis" section is a valuable addition, providing insights into the types of errors LLMs make during collaboration (e.g., premature initiation, repetitive initiation).

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Novelty (Over Overcooked-AI):** While the changes to Overcooked-AI are valuable, the underlying game and its core mechanics are not novel. The primary novelty lies in the *benchmark design* and the evaluation metrics. The level of contribution regarding novel environment design is not particularly substantial.
    *   **Complexity of Environment/Tasks:** The environment is grid-based, and the task space is relatively constrained. While this allows for controlled evaluation, it limits the generalizability of the findings to more complex, real-world collaborative scenarios. The "realism" of the tasks, while improved over Overcooked-AI, is still limited.
    *   **Reliance on Predefined RATs (Referential Action Trajectories):**  The evaluation heavily relies on pre-defined RATs. While this allows for a degree of objective evaluation, it may not fully capture the nuances of successful collaboration in dynamic environments where multiple valid strategies exist. The rigidity of predefined RATs can potentially penalize creative or alternative, yet successful, collaborative approaches.
    *   **Limited Evaluation of Communication Quality:** The paper focuses more on whether agents initiate/respond to collaboration and less on the *quality* of their communication. The content and clarity of the language used are not directly evaluated, which is a critical aspect of effective collaboration.
    *   **Baseline Weakness:** The description of the baseline is relatively brief. A stronger baseline would be beneficial for future research to compare against.

*   **Significance:**

    The paper's significance stems primarily from its focus on addressing a key weakness in how we evaluate collaborative LLM agents. By explicitly enforcing collaboration and introducing process-oriented metrics, it provides a more nuanced and informative assessment of these systems. The open-source nature of the benchmark further enhances its potential impact.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a worthwhile, but somewhat incremental contribution. It does not introduce any fundamentally new techniques or architectures. Instead, it refines an existing benchmark (Overcooked-AI) with a focus on better evaluating collaboration. The strengths of the paper are its clear problem statement, the introduction of relevant evaluation metrics, the enforced collaboration and the open source. The environment is not new, its simplicity is good for this type of analysis however it limits the generalizability of the results. While the approach has some weaknesses, particularly regarding the reliance on predefined RATs and limited assessment of communication quality, the benchmark itself is valuable. These weaknesses can also be improved through further investigations on the introduced benchmark, making its overall value higher.

### LongRoPE2: Near-Lossless LLM Context Window Scaling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20082v1)
- **Authors**: Ning Shang, Li Lyna Zhang, Siyuan Wang, Gaokai Zhang, Gilsinia Lopez, Fan Yang, Weizhu Chen, Mao Yang
- **Abstract**: LongRoPE2 is a novel approach that extends the effective context window of pre-trained large language models (LLMs) to the target length, while preserving the performance on the original shorter context window. This is achieved by three contributions: (1) a hypothesis that insufficient training in higher RoPE dimensions contributes to the persistent out-of-distribution (OOD) issues observed in existing methods; (2) an effective RoPE rescaling algorithm that adopts evolutionary search guided by "needle-driven" perplexity to address the insufficient training problem; (3) a mixed context window training approach that fine-tunes model weights to adopt rescaled RoPE for long-context sequences while preserving the short-context performance with the original RoPE. Extensive experiments on LLaMA3-8B and Phi3-mini-3.8B across various benchmarks validate the hypothesis and demonstrate the effectiveness of LongRoPE2. Remarkably, LongRoPE2 extends LLaMA3-8B to achieve a 128K effective context length while retaining over 98.5% of short-context performance, using only 10B tokens -- 80x fewer than Meta's approach, which fails to reach the target effective context length. Code will be available at https://github.com/microsoft/LongRoPE.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the LongRoPE2 paper:

**Summary:**

The LongRoPE2 paper introduces a novel approach for extending the context window of pre-trained Large Language Models (LLMs) while preserving performance on shorter contexts. The core contributions are: 1) the hypothesis that under-training in higher RoPE dimensions contributes to Out-of-Distribution (OOD) issues, 2) a RoPE rescaling algorithm using evolutionary search guided by "needle-driven" perplexity to address this, and 3) a mixed context window training approach to fine-tune model weights for rescaled RoPE while preserving original performance. Extensive experiments on LLaMA3-8B and Phi3-mini-3.8B demonstrate the effectiveness of LongRoPE2, achieving a 128k effective context length with minimal short-context performance degradation and significantly less training data compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel components. The "needle-driven" perplexity evaluation is a valuable innovation, as it focuses on evaluating performance on tokens requiring long-range dependencies instead of averaging over all tokens in the long document. This allows for better capture of the actual long-context performance. Furthermore, the evolutionary search component, in combination with a better way to determine the true critical dimensions is novel. This enables the algorithm to learn better scaling factors. Finally, the mixed context window training, enabling a long-context and short-context to exist, allows to address issues regarding short-context degradation.

*   **Significance:** Extending context windows is a crucial area of LLM research. LongRoPE2 addresses a significant limitation of existing techniques: performance degradation on shorter contexts. The paper's claim of near-lossless performance, especially with reduced training data compared to approaches like Meta's 800B token mid-training of LLaMA3.1-8B, is significant. Additionally, the paper provides a detailed analysis of the OOD problem in RoPE and provides insight into why current methods can't address the issues well.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong empirical results:** The paper demonstrates performance improvements across various benchmarks, synthetic tests (RULER, Needle in a Haystack), and real-world datasets (LOFT, InfiniteBench, LongBench). The result on achieving 128k context window with retaining high short context capability are highly significant
    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing RoPE rescaling methods.
    *   **Well-motivated approach:** The needle-driven perplexity evaluation and mixed context window training are logically motivated and address specific shortcomings of previous methods.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors promise to release their code, enabling reproducibility and further research.
    *   **Significantly reduced training cost:** This work shows a large performance jump while requiring less data to train, which makes the work economically feasible.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limitations of needle-driven perplexity:** While the approach is novel, the needle-driven perplexity score could be sensitive to the "needle" selected. A poorly chosen needle could lead to suboptimal rescaling factors. More analysis is needed to prove that the data set is not biased.
    *   **Limited scope of datasets:** While the experiments are extensive, it'd be valuable to see results on a broader set of real-world datasets, especially those focusing on very long document understanding, such as summarization.
    *   **The results on the LongBench are not great**: The results on LongBench are not particularly great, and can be improved with more data.
    *   **The impact of mixed context window training not clearly dissected**: it would have been a better paper if there was an ablation on mixed context window training and not just a "no mixed context window training" setting. It would have been useful to see how much each component contributed.

*   **Potential Influence:** LongRoPE2 has the potential to become a widely adopted technique for context window extension.  The near-lossless performance and reduced training cost are compelling advantages. The insights regarding RoPE OOD issues can also influence future research in positional embeddings. Finally, since it helps models maintain performance on various real-world tasks, it improves current LLMs which benefit users

*   **Score Rationale:** Based on the novelty of the technical contributions (needle-driven perplexity and mixed context window training), the strong empirical validation, and the potential influence on the field, but also considering the identified weaknesses (sensitivity to needle selection, limited scope of datasets), a score of 8 is appropriate. While impactful, there is room for further refinement. The score reflects the advancement beyond prior art, but acknowledges the need for future work.

### Generative augmentations for improved cardiac ultrasound segmentation using diffusion models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20100v1)
- **Authors**: Gilles Van De Vyver, Aksel Try Lenz, Erik Smistad, Sindre Hellum Olaisen, Bjørnar Grenne, Espen Holte, Håavard Dalen, Lasse Løvstakken
- **Abstract**: One of the main challenges in current research on segmentation in cardiac ultrasound is the lack of large and varied labeled datasets and the differences in annotation conventions between datasets. This makes it difficult to design robust segmentation models that generalize well to external datasets. This work utilizes diffusion models to create generative augmentations that can significantly improve diversity of the dataset and thus the generalisability of segmentation models without the need for more annotated data. The augmentations are applied in addition to regular augmentations. A visual test survey showed that experts cannot clearly distinguish between real and fully generated images. Using the proposed generative augmentations, segmentation robustness was increased when training on an internal dataset and testing on an external dataset with an improvement of over 20 millimeters in Hausdorff distance. Additionally, the limits of agreement for automatic ejection fraction estimation improved by up to 20% of absolute ejection fraction value on out of distribution cases. These improvements come exclusively from the increased variation of the training data using the generative augmentations, without modifying the underlying machine learning model. The augmentation tool is available as an open source Python library at https://github.com/GillesVanDeVyver/EchoGAINS.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel approach to improve cardiac ultrasound segmentation by using generative augmentations based on diffusion models. The core idea is to train a diffusion model unconditionally on ultrasound images (or a dataset with different annotation conventions) and then use it to generate more diverse training data for a segmentation model. The generative model uses the RePaint technique, allowing for targeted augmentation of the image surroundings while preserving the original, accurate segmentations. The authors show that this approach improves segmentation robustness, particularly when training on a limited dataset (HUNT4Echo) and testing on a more diverse one (CAMUS). The paper also includes a human evaluation showing that the generated images are difficult to distinguish from real ones, even by expert cardiologists. Finally, they evaluate the impact of these augmentations on automatic ejection fraction (EF) estimation, demonstrating improved limits of agreement.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of cardiac ultrasound segmentation, addressing a critical challenge: the scarcity and limited diversity of labeled data. The use of unconditional diffusion models and RePaint for generative augmentation is a compelling strategy for several reasons:

*   **Addresses Data Scarcity and Domain Shift:** By generating augmentations, the method effectively increases the size and diversity of the training dataset, mitigating the effects of limited data availability and domain shifts between datasets acquired with different protocols or annotations.
*   **Preserves Accurate Segmentations:** The RePaint approach ensures that augmentations primarily affect the image surroundings, avoiding inconsistencies and artifacts in the critical region of interest with the segmentation mask.
*   **Generative Model Independence:** The use of an *unconditional* diffusion model means it can be trained independently on even unlabeled data (or data with mismatched labels), making it particularly useful in scenarios where labeled data is scarce or costly to obtain.

However, several aspects of the paper warrant critical attention:

*   **Incremental Novelty:** While the application of diffusion models and RePaint to cardiac ultrasound segmentation is novel, the individual techniques are well-established. The paper's novelty primarily lies in the specific combination and application of these existing techniques to a particular problem.
*   **Limited Analysis of Failure Modes:** While the paper includes a survey of the generated images and an error analysis on the EF calculations, a more thorough investigation into the failure cases of the segmentation model after augmentation is warranted. Understanding the types of segmentation errors that persist despite the augmentations would be valuable. Showing an example of a diffusion model output with segmentation mask overlayed would be useful.
*   **Limited Clinical Evaluation:** The improvement in EF estimation is promising, but the clinical impact of the improved segmentation robustness needs to be further validated with larger clinical datasets and in real-world clinical scenarios. What is the consequence of the EF estimation bias? What is the variation in heart rate in the dataset.
*   **Choice of Datasets:** The contrast between HUNT4Echo and CAMUS datasets is valid, but a more detailed explanation of their inherent differences (beyond image acquisition) is needed. The paper assumes LV-focused images are worse, but clinical images of the heart must show more than just the LV.

**Strengths:**

*   Clear and well-written.
*   Addresses a relevant problem in cardiac ultrasound.
*   Demonstrates practical improvements in segmentation robustness.
*   The use of human evaluation strengthens the results.
*   Open-source code makes the method accessible.

**Weaknesses:**

*   Incremental novelty.
*   Limited analysis of failure modes after augmentation.
*   Requires computationally expensive pre-training of generative model.

**Significance:**

The paper demonstrates a promising approach to improving cardiac ultrasound segmentation. The ability to leverage unlabeled data and generate realistic augmentations has the potential to significantly reduce the reliance on expensive and time-consuming manual annotations. While the paper's novelty is somewhat incremental, the practical impact and potential for widespread adoption make it a valuable contribution to the field.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses outlined above, the paper demonstrates a practical application of existing techniques with tangible benefits in the context of cardiac ultrasound segmentation. While the method’s novelty is not groundbreaking, it significantly improves generalization when dealing with limited labeled data. A more in-depth analysis of the failure modes and a more rigorous clinical evaluation are needed for this work to have an impact on the clinical setting.

### VDT-Auto: End-to-end Autonomous Driving with VLM-Guided Diffusion Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20108v1)
- **Authors**: Ziang Guo, Konstantin Gubernatorov, Selamawit Asfaw, Zakhar Yagudin, Dzmitry Tsetserukou
- **Abstract**: In autonomous driving, dynamic environment and corner cases pose significant challenges to the robustness of ego vehicle's decision-making. To address these challenges, commencing with the representation of state-action mapping in the end-to-end autonomous driving paradigm, we introduce a novel pipeline, VDT-Auto. Leveraging the advancement of the state understanding of Visual Language Model (VLM), incorporating with diffusion Transformer-based action generation, our VDT-Auto parses the environment geometrically and contextually for the conditioning of the diffusion process. Geometrically, we use a bird's-eye view (BEV) encoder to extract feature grids from the surrounding images. Contextually, the structured output of our fine-tuned VLM is processed into textual embeddings and noisy paths. During our diffusion process, the added noise for the forward process is sampled from the noisy path output of the fine-tuned VLM, while the extracted BEV feature grids and embedded texts condition the reverse process of our diffusion Transformers. Our VDT-Auto achieved 0.52m on average L2 errors and 21% on average collision rate in the nuScenes open-loop planning evaluation. Moreover, the real-world demonstration exhibited prominent generalizability of our VDT-Auto. The code and dataset will be released after acceptance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "VDT-Auto: End-to-end Autonomous Driving with VLM-Guided Diffusion Transformers":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VDT-Auto, a novel end-to-end autonomous driving pipeline that leverages Visual Language Models (VLMs) and diffusion Transformers. VDT-Auto uses a BEV encoder to extract geometric features from surrounding images, and a fine-tuned VLM to capture contextual information by analyzing a front image and generating textual descriptions, driving advice, and path proposals.  These geometric features and contextual embeddings condition diffusion Transformers which predict optimized paths for the ego vehicle. The authors demonstrate the method's effectiveness in the nuScenes open-loop planning evaluation and showcase its promising generalization capabilities in real-world driving scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Integration of VLMs for contextual understanding:** The use of a VLM to extract contextual information about the driving scene is a strong point. It addresses a key limitation of purely geometric approaches by incorporating semantic understanding, allowing the system to reason about the scene in a more human-like manner. This integration contributes to the potential for improved safety and decision-making.
    *   **Diffusion Transformers for action generation:** The use of diffusion Transformers to generate the optimized path is a relatively novel approach in end-to-end driving. The process of sampling noise from the VLM-generated path proposals for diffusion is clever and helps condition the trajectory generation on contextual advice.
    *   **Differentiable Pipeline & Potential for End-to-End Training:** The differentiability of the architecture is a key benefit. The promise of end-to-end training would allow for further optimization and adaptation to diverse driving scenarios.
    *   **Real-world demonstration:** The real-world demonstration provides valuable evidence of the method's generalizability to unseen environments.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited quantitative results in the real-world demonstration:** The real-world results are qualitative and lack quantitative metrics. It is difficult to assess the true performance of the system in complex, real-world scenarios without detailed metrics, especially concerning safety aspects (e.g., collision avoidance, adherence to traffic rules).
    *   **Dependence on VLM quality:** The performance of VDT-Auto is heavily reliant on the quality of the fine-tuned VLM. Errors or biases in the VLM's perception and reasoning can directly translate to suboptimal or even unsafe driving behavior.
    *   **Limited novelty in components:** While the overall architecture is novel, many individual components (BEV encoder, diffusion Transformers) are based on existing research. The innovation primarily lies in the integration and conditioning strategy.
    *   **Limited ablation study:** While some ablation results are shown, more comprehensive experiments are needed. For instance, different VLM configurations and fine-tuning techniques for the VLM component can be analysed in more detail.
    *   **Open-loop evaluation:** The nuScenes evaluation is performed in open-loop setting which does not fully capture the closed-loop interaction between planning and execution. A closed-loop evaluation would provide a more realistic assessment of the system's performance in dynamic scenarios.
    *   **Lack of comparative analysis with VLMs alone:** There's no direct comparison to a simpler approach using only VLM outputs to drive the vehicle. It's unclear how much improvement the diffusion transformer adds over using just the contextual information and path proposals generated by the VLM.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper explores a promising avenue for improving autonomous driving systems by combining geometric perception with contextual understanding from VLMs. This direction has significant potential for addressing the challenges posed by complex and dynamic driving environments.
    *   The VDT-Auto pipeline contributes to the ongoing research in end-to-end autonomous driving, moving towards more integrated and intelligent systems. The framework is adaptable to future advancements in VLMs and diffusion models.
    *   The release of the code and dataset (as promised) would contribute significantly to the research community.

**Overall Score:**

Given the combination of novel architecture integrating geometric and contextual understanding, the use of diffusion transformers, and the real-world demonstration, but also considering the limitations of the evaluation and the reliance on the underlying VLM, a score of **6** is appropriate. The integration of VLMs for scene understanding is a promising direction, but the paper needs more rigorous quantitative evaluation and a more detailed comparative analysis against other existing methods to solidify its significance. While novel, the core components are based on existing techniques, lessening the impact. Future research building upon this framework could significantly increase its impact.

### Scalability of the second-order reliability method for stochastic differential equations with multiplicative noise
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20114v1)
- **Authors**: Timo Schorlepp, Tobias Grafke
- **Abstract**: We show how to efficiently compute asymptotically sharp estimates of extreme event probabilities in stochastic differential equations (SDEs) with small multiplicative Brownian noise. The underlying approximation is known as sharp large deviation theory or precise Laplace asymptotics in mathematics, the second-order reliability method (SORM) in reliability engineering, and the instanton or optimal fluctuation method with 1-loop corrections in physics. It is based on approximating the tail probability in question with the most probable realization of the stochastic process, and local perturbations around this realization. We first recall and contextualize the relevant classical theoretical result on precise Laplace asymptotics of diffusion processes [Ben Arous (1988), Stochastics, 25(3), 125-153], and then show how to compute the involved infinite-dimensional quantities - operator traces and Carleman-Fredholm determinants - numerically in a way that is scalable with respect to the time discretization and remains feasible in high spatial dimensions. Using tools from automatic differentiation, we achieve a straightforward black-box numerical computation of the SORM estimates in JAX. The method is illustrated in examples of SDEs and stochastic partial differential equations, including a two-dimensional random advection-diffusion model of a passive scalar. We thereby demonstrate that it is possible to obtain efficient and accurate SORM estimates for very high-dimensional problems, as long as the infinite-dimensional structure of the problem is correctly taken into account. Our JAX implementation of the method is made publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper extends the Second-Order Reliability Method (SORM), a technique for approximating extreme event probabilities, to stochastic differential equations (SDEs) with multiplicative noise. It addresses the scalability issues that arise when naively applying SORM to discretized SDEs with multiplicative noise. The key contribution is a modified SORM estimate, based on the precise Laplace asymptotics of diffusion processes, involving Carleman-Fredholm determinants and regularized operator traces. The authors demonstrate how to efficiently compute these quantities numerically in a scalable manner using automatic differentiation in JAX, making SORM feasible for high-dimensional problems like stochastic PDEs (specifically, a 2D advection-diffusion equation). The paper provides a theoretical justification and illustrates the method with examples, demonstrating its improved scalability and accuracy compared to naive approaches. They make their JAX implementation publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper's strength lies in its practical contribution: providing a *scalable* algorithm for approximating rare event probabilities in SDEs with multiplicative noise. While the theoretical underpinnings (precise Laplace asymptotics from Ben Arous, 1988) are not entirely new, the paper successfully bridges the gap between the theoretical result and a practical computational method capable of handling high-dimensional problems. The use of automatic differentiation in JAX is a clever and effective way to handle the complex calculations of derivatives. The emphasis on a matrix-free implementation directly addresses the scalability bottleneck. The code release enhances reproducibility and adoption.

However, several aspects warrant critical consideration:

*   **Novelty:** The core theoretical result builds on existing work (Ben Arous, 1988 and references therein). The theoretical contribution here is largely in reformulating the relevant results in a way amenable to numerical computation *and* identifying the renormalization requirement. Furthermore, the use of similar techniques in similar models has also recently been reported.

*   **Practical Limitations:** Although the authors demonstrate scalability, the method still requires solving optimization problems to find the instanton. The success of the method depends on the well-posedness and non-degeneracy of this optimization problem. In very complex or chaotic systems, finding the global minimizer might be computationally challenging or even impossible. The assumptions of a unique and nondegenerate minimizer are strong. The paper acknowledges this, but it's a significant limitation.

*   **Benchmarking:** While the examples are useful, the quantitative benefit compared to other rare event simulation techniques (e.g., importance sampling or splitting) are not extensively demonstrated (at least in terms of computational time and accuracy.) That said, comparing to crude Monte Carlo simulation is shown, and demonstrates that the scalability of the asymptotic estimate permits a reasonable performance comparison to even mild estimates of rare event probabilities. Additionally, comparisons with "naive" approaches clearly demonstrates the utility of the renormalized formulation.

*   **Scope of Applicability:** The method relies on specific assumptions about the SDE (smoothness and non-degeneracy). While many real-world problems fit into this framework, there are also scenarios where these assumptions might not hold. It would strengthen the paper to explicitly discuss strategies for diagnosing or addressing violations of these assumptions.

*   **Clarity of exposition:** Although appendices provide a background on the formal machinery, the exposition may be rather inaccessible to those unfamiliar with the tools and notation of large deviation theory and calculus on Wiener space.

**Significance:**

The paper represents a significant advancement in the practical applicability of SORM and related methods. It successfully addresses a crucial scalability issue in the context of SDEs with multiplicative noise, which are prevalent in many scientific and engineering applications. The open-source JAX implementation provides a readily available tool for researchers and practitioners to explore and apply SORM in their respective fields. This could lead to a wider adoption of asymptotic approximation techniques for rare event analysis. The use of a randomized trace estimate and other scalable techniques, while not always necessary for low state-space dimensions, could offer a useful tool for stochastic partial differential equations.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper is not groundbreaking in its theoretical contribution, since the major pieces are derived from previous publications, but addresses a major scalability bottleneck preventing the general deployment of Second Order Reliability Method (SORM) in practice. The presented algorithm offers a clear advantage (i.e. scalability) relative to previous, naive implementations of SORM to estimate extreme event statistics. Furthermore, open-sourcing the JAX implementation should lower the barrier to future research and applications, such as stochastic partial differential equations, or uncertainty quantification frameworks. The score has been slightly lowered due to: the reliance on a unique global minimum that is guaranteed to have particular properties, limited comparisons to state-of-the-art computational methods, the relative inaccessibility of the material.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Self-Training Elicits Concise Reasoning in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20122v1)
- **Authors**: Tergel Munkhbat, Namgyu Ho, Seohyun Kim, Yongjin Yang, Yujin Kim, Se-Young Yun
- **Abstract**: Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning has enabled large language models (LLMs) to utilize additional computation through intermediate tokens to solve complex tasks. However, we posit that typical reasoning traces contain many redundant tokens, incurring extraneous inference costs. Upon examination of the output distribution of current LLMs, we find evidence on their latent ability to reason more concisely, relative to their default behavior. To elicit this capability, we propose simple fine-tuning methods which leverage self-generated concise reasoning paths obtained by best-of-N sampling and few-shot conditioning, in task-specific settings. Our combined method achieves a 30% reduction in output tokens on average, across five model families on GSM8K and MATH, while maintaining average accuracy. By exploiting the fundamental stochasticity and in-context learning capabilities of LLMs, our self-training approach robustly elicits concise reasoning on a wide range of models, including those with extensive post-training. Code is available at https://github.com/TergelMunkhbat/concise-reasoning
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Self-Training Elicits Concise Reasoning in Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the redundancy in the reasoning chains generated by Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors posit that LLMs often generate more tokens than necessary for solving complex tasks, leading to extraneous inference costs. They propose a self-training method to elicit more concise reasoning by leveraging best-of-N sampling and few-shot conditioning in task-specific settings. The method involves generating multiple reasoning paths for each training question, selecting the shortest correct path, and fine-tuning the LLM on this curated dataset. This approach achieves a reduction in output tokens (around 30% on average across GSM8K and MATH) while maintaining accuracy. They also show that the trained model can adaptively adjust the output length based on the question's complexity. The method proves effective across a wide range of model families, including models with extensive post-training.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of encouraging conciseness in LLM reasoning through self-training is moderately novel. While the observations about redundancy in CoT are not entirely new, systematically tackling it using the specific combination of best-of-N sampling, few-shot conditioning, and fine-tuning has not been extensively explored. Existing works have touched upon either prompting for conciseness or fine-tuning based on other metrics, but the paper presents a more holistic and data-driven approach. The adaptive reasoning length based on question complexity, while intuitive, is a valuable finding empirically demonstrated by the study.
*   **Significance:** The potential impact of this work is significant. Reducing the number of tokens generated by LLMs directly translates to reduced inference costs, lower latency, and improved scalability. This is particularly important for deploying LLMs in resource-constrained environments or real-time applications. While a 30% reduction is substantial, the paper would have been strengthened with an inference cost analysis (e.g., latency improvements). The approach of selectively leveraging more efficient examples from within a model's existing output distribution is also compelling for transfer learning.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides strong empirical evidence to support its claims. The evaluation is thorough, covering multiple datasets (GSM8K and MATH), a range of model families (Llama, Gemma, Qwen), and ablation studies. The results consistently show that the proposed method achieves significant token reduction without sacrificing accuracy.
    *   **Simplicity and Generality:** The method is relatively simple to implement and can be readily applied to different LLMs and tasks. It only relies on self-generated data, making it adaptable without external annotations.
    *   **Analysis:** The paper provides insightful analysis of the reasoning length distribution and explores the effect of augmentation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scale:** While tested on multiple moderately sized models, the evaluation of extremely large LLMs (100B+ parameters) would provide further insights into the scaling behavior of the proposed method.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical Grounding:** The paper is primarily empirical and lacks a deeper theoretical explanation of why the proposed method works. It would be beneficial to have a more formal analysis of the underlying mechanisms of conciseness elicitation.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** While a limited set of ablations are included, a deeper exploration of the trade-offs between the different components (BoN sampling, few-shot conditioning, self-training iterations) and the effects of different N values is crucial.
    *   **Metrics:** Inference costs, including latency numbers could have been included in the tables and discussions.
    *   **Limited discussion on failure mode:** Discussing limitations on specific types of questions that may not benefit is important for future work.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several directions. It can motivate further exploration of self-training techniques for improving LLM efficiency, lead to the development of more efficient reasoning architectures, and inspire the design of training objectives that explicitly promote conciseness. It also provides a practical recipe for deploying more cost-effective LLMs in real-world applications.

**Conclusion:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution by demonstrating an effective and relatively simple method for eliciting concise reasoning in LLMs through self-training. The empirical results are compelling, and the method has the potential to significantly reduce inference costs without compromising accuracy. Despite some limitations, the paper makes a practical improvement over existing solutions and unlocks more efficient LLM applications in a variety of domains.
