- **Score**: 7/10

### AI Will Always Love You: Studying Implicit Biases in Romantic AI Companions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20231v1)
- **Authors**: Clare Grogan, Jackie Kay, María Pérez-Ortiz
- **Abstract**: While existing studies have recognised explicit biases in generative models, including occupational gender biases, the nuances of gender stereotypes and expectations of relationships between users and AI companions remain underexplored. In the meantime, AI companions have become increasingly popular as friends or gendered romantic partners to their users. This study bridges the gap by devising three experiments tailored for romantic, gender-assigned AI companions and their users, effectively evaluating implicit biases across various-sized LLMs. Each experiment looks at a different dimension: implicit associations, emotion responses, and sycophancy. This study aims to measure and compare biases manifested in different companion systems by quantitatively analysing persona-assigned model responses to a baseline through newly devised metrics. The results are noteworthy: they show that assigning gendered, relationship personas to Large Language Models significantly alters the responses of these models, and in certain situations in a biased, stereotypical way.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a concise summary of the paper and a critical evaluation with a score.

**Summary:**

The paper "AI Will Always Love You: Studying Implicit Biases in Romantic AI Companions" investigates implicit biases in large language models (LLMs) when assigned gendered and relationship-specific personas, particularly within the context of abusive and controlling relationship dynamics. The authors design three experiments: an adapted Implicit Association Test (IAT), an emotion response evaluation, and a sycophancy measurement. They examine how LLMs respond differently when prompted as a "girlfriend," "husband," etc., and how user personas influence these responses. Their findings indicate that assigning gendered and relationship personas does significantly alter LLM responses, sometimes in biased and stereotypical ways. They find varying results, depending on the models used and scenarios presented, showing that the larger models tend to have increased biases and that male assigned models tend to select anger more often.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The paper does address a relatively underexplored area: the interplay between relationship roles, gender assignment, and implicit biases in LLMs, *specifically* looking at abusive relationships. While studies on gender bias and persona biases in LLMs exist (cited by the authors), the focus on romantic AI companions *with that specific harmful dynamic* is a differentiator. However, the application of existing bias measurement techniques (IAT) and the modifications made for LLMs, are not groundbreaking. Therefore, the methodological novelty is limited.

**Significance:**  The work's significance stems from the growing popularity of AI companions and the potential for these systems to perpetuate or amplify harmful biases in relationships.  Highlighting these biases is important for ethical AI development and user safety.  Identifying that relationship titles could skew the results of an LLM presents a new risk that needed to be addressed. Also, the focus on a specific scenario of abusive/controlling relations adds a critical element that current research on gender biases and AI do not typically address.

**Strengths:**

*   **Relevant and timely topic:** The rising use of AI companions makes this research socially relevant.
*   **Well-defined research questions:** The research questions are clear and guide the experiments effectively.
*   **Combination of Methods:** Using three different experiments (IAT, emotion response, sycophancy) allows for a more holistic analysis.
*   **Reproducibility:** Code and Results are available.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Methodological Innovation:**  The core methodologies rely on adaptations of established techniques. The changes made to the experiments for use on LLMs do not have sufficient details on *why* they were designed as they were, and therefore does not have the proper justifications for making these choices.
*   **Oversimplification of Complex Relationships:** While focusing on abuse is valuable, the scenarios and prompts might be too simplistic to fully capture the nuances of complex relationships and the subtleties of abusive dynamics. Real abusive dynamics can also involve economic abuse and other types of manipulation that is very hard to capture here.
*   **Ambiguous Results and Limited Generalizability:** Some results are inconsistent across models and experimental conditions, making it difficult to draw firm conclusions. It's difficult to know how the patterns translate into how people will use and respond to chatbots.
*   **Reliance on Limited Stimuli:** The list of scenarios are very limited and may not be comprehensive enough to be representative.
*   **Limited Metrics and Interpretations:** Reliance on percentages could be misleading, since some of the responses and interactions were also limited and the samples could be too small. Also, the reasoning behind why some metrics are used is unclear.
*   **Potentially Conflated Concepts:** While the authors try to measure specific dimensions of bias, there could be significant overlap and correlation between "sycophancy," "emotional alignment," and simple stereotypical responses.

**Potential Influence:**  The paper can raise awareness and stimulate further research into ethical considerations for AI companion development. It can also inform design choices for building safer and less biased AI relationship systems. This is important as we are still in the early days of human-AI relationships.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper addresses an important and timely topic with relevant experiments. However, the limited methodological innovation, and limited strength in the result makes it that the impact is more limited than it could be.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Attention Distillation: A Unified Approach to Visual Characteristics Transfer
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20235v1)
- **Authors**: Yang Zhou, Xu Gao, Zichong Chen, Hui Huang
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in generative diffusion models have shown a notable inherent understanding of image style and semantics. In this paper, we leverage the self-attention features from pretrained diffusion networks to transfer the visual characteristics from a reference to generated images. Unlike previous work that uses these features as plug-and-play attributes, we propose a novel attention distillation loss calculated between the ideal and current stylization results, based on which we optimize the synthesized image via backpropagation in latent space. Next, we propose an improved Classifier Guidance that integrates attention distillation loss into the denoising sampling process, further accelerating the synthesis and enabling a broad range of image generation applications. Extensive experiments have demonstrated the extraordinary performance of our approach in transferring the examples' style, appearance, and texture to new images in synthesis. Code is available at https://github.com/xugao97/AttentionDistillation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Attention Distillation: A Unified Approach to Visual Characteristics Transfer":

**Summary:**

This paper introduces "Attention Distillation" (AD), a novel loss function for transferring visual characteristics from a reference image to a generated image using pre-trained diffusion models. The approach leverages self-attention features within the diffusion model's denoising networks. Unlike previous plug-and-play methods that directly inject these features, AD optimizes the synthesized image through backpropagation in the latent space, aiming to minimize the difference between the ideal (re-aggregated reference KV features with target Q) and the current stylization attention outputs. The method can also be integrated into diffusion sampling as an improved Classifier Guidance mechanism. The authors demonstrate the method's effectiveness across various image synthesis applications, including style transfer, appearance transfer, style-specific text-to-image generation, and texture synthesis.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Unified Framework:** The paper's main strength is presenting a single, unified framework that can handle a wide range of visual characteristic transfer tasks. This is a significant advantage over methods that are tailored to specific applications.
    *   **Novel Loss Function:** The proposed Attention Distillation loss is a novel approach. The idea of using backpropagation to directly optimize the latent space based on the difference between attention outputs is interesting and shows good results.
    *   **Integration with Diffusion Models:** The integration of AD into the diffusion sampling process as a modified Classifier Guidance is a clever way to accelerate synthesis and expand the applicability of the method. It also facilitates controllable generation (e.g., using ControlNet).
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper presents extensive experimental results with qualitative comparisons against state-of-the-art methods. The visual results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method in preserving style details and semantic structures compared to competing approaches.
    *   **Addressing Limitations:** The paper acknowledges and attempts to address the limitations of existing plug-and-play attention feature transfer methods, namely: the domain gap, error accumulation, and architectural limits.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty (Potentially):** While AD loss is novel in its implementation, the underlying concept of leveraging self-attention features for style/texture transfer is *not entirely new*. Several recent works have explored this avenue. The true novelty lies in the distillation loss formulation and its incorporation in both optimization and guided sampling within diffusion model. A very clear comparison in the related work is necessary to fully show it's innovation.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Depth:** While the paper introduces the AD loss, it lacks a deeper theoretical analysis of *why* this particular loss function works so well. Understanding its convergence properties, sensitivity to hyperparameters, and robustness to different dataset characteristics would strengthen the contribution. The experimental section also lack detail of the effect of hyperparameter choices.
    *   **Reproducibility Concerns:** Implementation details, such as the specific layers of the UNet used for AD loss calculation, the exact training regime for finetuning the VAE decoder, the way the text is used in a text to image pipeline are often vague.
    *   **Clarity and Exposition:** While the paper is generally well-written, some sections (especially the one regarding the improved VAE decoding) could benefit from more clarity and a detailed explanation of how it affects the diffusion model.
    *   **Over Claims on "Unity":** While the method *can* be used for all the presented tasks, it is often far away from a true "unity" in terms of parameters and settings. The different components (e.g. attention distillation guided sampling with or without ControlNet, etc.) need to be tested on the applications where only a single "unified" method can be deployed.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper introduces a practical and effective technique that can be readily adopted by the research community.
    *   The unified framework simplifies the process of visual characteristic transfer across diverse applications.
    *   The method has the potential to influence future research in controllable image synthesis and style transfer.
    *   The experimental data provides clear results and is carefully evaluated.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, a score of **7/10** is appropriate.

*   The paper presents a *significant* engineering contribution in the form of the AD loss and the way it integrates with diffusion models, leading to performance gains across several image synthesis tasks.
*   The paper's novelty, while existing, is not revolutionary. It is more an improvement over previous methods which themselves relied on the same core ideas. The significance of its novelty depends largely on whether or not related method could adapt such an improvement and achieve similiar results.
*   The extensive experimental results and the unified framework justify its value to the community.
*   The lack of deeper theoretical analysis and exposition, as well as potential reproduciblity issues prevent a higher score.

The paper is a valuable contribution to the field of image synthesis but falls short of being truly groundbreaking. Further theoretical analysis, clarity in presentation, as well as a stronger validation to a more general setting would significantly increase its impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 0/10

### Teasing Apart Architecture and Initial Weights as Sources of Inductive Bias in Neural Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20237v1)
- **Authors**: Gianluca Bencomo, Max Gupta, Ioana Marinescu, R. Thomas McCoy, Thomas L. Griffiths
- **Abstract**: Artificial neural networks can acquire many aspects of human knowledge from data, making them promising as models of human learning. But what those networks can learn depends upon their inductive biases -- the factors other than the data that influence the solutions they discover -- and the inductive biases of neural networks remain poorly understood, limiting our ability to draw conclusions about human learning from the performance of these systems. Cognitive scientists and machine learning researchers often focus on the architecture of a neural network as a source of inductive bias. In this paper we explore the impact of another source of inductive bias -- the initial weights of the network -- using meta-learning as a tool for finding initial weights that are adapted for specific problems. We evaluate four widely-used architectures -- MLPs, CNNs, LSTMs, and Transformers -- by meta-training 430 different models across three tasks requiring different biases and forms of generalization. We find that meta-learning can substantially reduce or entirely eliminate performance differences across architectures and data representations, suggesting that these factors may be less important as sources of inductive bias than is typically assumed. When differences are present, architectures and data representations that perform well without meta-learning tend to meta-train more effectively. Moreover, all architectures generalize poorly on problems that are far from their meta-training experience, underscoring the need for stronger inductive biases for robust generalization.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary of the paper and a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance.

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the inductive biases of neural networks, specifically examining the relative contributions of network architecture and initial weights (optimized via meta-learning) in shaping learning outcomes. The authors meta-train four different architectures (MLP, CNN, LSTM, Transformer) on three tasks (concept learning, modular arithmetic, few-shot Omniglot) and compare their performance to randomly initialized baselines. The key finding is that meta-learning can significantly reduce or eliminate performance differences between architectures and data representations, suggesting that architecture may be a less dominant source of inductive bias than often assumed. However, all architectures struggle with out-of-distribution tasks, highlighting the need for stronger inductive biases for robust generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic comparison of architecture vs. meta-learned initial weights across multiple architectures and tasks.  While meta-learning for inductive bias is not entirely new, the direct comparison across diverse architectural choices and contrasting against non-meta-learned counterparts is a valuable contribution. It provides a more comprehensive perspective than previous studies that usually focus on a specific architecture. The exploration of both in-distribution and out-of-distribution generalization is also a positive aspect.

**Significance:**  The paper's findings have implications for how we understand the role of architectural choices in neural network design. If initial weights can be optimized to largely override architectural biases, it challenges the common assumption that carefully designed architectures are *always* essential for specific tasks. This suggests that a more flexible, meta-trained approach to neural network development could be viable, where architecture becomes less critical and the focus shifts towards optimizing initial weights and learning algorithms. However, the paper also highlights the importance of architecture in OOD settings, which suggests that the paper demonstrates *when* architecture matters. This has significance for model design and development of more robust AI systems. The paper also underscores the importance of careful experimental design and baselines when evaluating novel meta-learning techniques.

**Strengths:**

*   **Systematic Comparison:** The paper presents a well-designed and controlled experimental framework that systematically explores architecture vs. initial weights via meta-learning.
*   **Diverse Architectures and Tasks:** Using a variety of architectures and tasks increases the generalizability of the results.
*   **Clear Baselines:** Comparing to randomly initialized networks provides a clear understanding of the benefits of meta-learning.
*   **Addresses Limitations:** The discussion of out-of-distribution generalization and the limitations of meta-learning provides a balanced perspective.
*   **Good visualizations**: Visualizing how models fit the data helps highlight some of the nuances of the methods, providing additional intuition for the results.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Task Complexity:** The tasks used, while diverse, are relatively simple compared to real-world applications. It's unclear whether the findings would hold for more complex tasks with higher-dimensional data and intricate dependencies.
*   **Meta-Learning Method Specificity:** The results might be specific to Meta-SGD. While Meta-SGD provides a strong optimization framework, there is a growing range of options for meta-learning that may yield different results.
*   **Computational Cost:** Meta-training is computationally expensive, limiting the scale of the architectures and the number of tasks that can be explored.
*   **Unexplained architectural advantage**: It seems, at least in the few-shot learning tasks, CNNs retain more of the original accuracy over other architectures when data is out of distribution. The exact reason for this architectural difference is not explained.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a valuable and well-executed study that challenges conventional wisdom regarding the importance of architecture in neural networks. While the limitations related to task complexity and meta-learning method specificity are worth noting, the systematic comparison across architectures and tasks, the clear baselines, and the balanced discussion of limitations make this a significant contribution. The work encourages researchers to reconsider the relationship between architecture, initialization, and learning algorithms in neural networks. The insights help inform the development of more flexible and robust AI systems.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper is a solid and well-executed contribution to the field. It's not a revolutionary breakthrough, but it presents valuable insights and challenges existing assumptions in a clear and systematic way. While the limitations, especially those related to task complexity and meta-learning method, prevent a higher score, the work has the potential to influence future research in meta-learning and neural network design.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### FINEREASON: Evaluating and Improving LLMs' Deliberate Reasoning through Reflective Puzzle Solving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20238v1)
- **Authors**: Guizhen Chen, Weiwen Xu, Hao Zhang, Hou Pong Chan, Chaoqun Liu, Lidong Bing, Deli Zhao, Anh Tuan Luu, Yu Rong
- **Abstract**: Many challenging reasoning tasks require not just rapid, intuitive responses, but a more deliberate, multi-step approach. Recent progress in large language models (LLMs) highlights an important shift from the "System 1" way of quick reactions to the "System 2" style of reflection-and-correction problem solving. However, current benchmarks heavily rely on the final-answer accuracy, leaving much of a model's intermediate reasoning steps unexamined. This fails to assess the model's ability to reflect and rectify mistakes within the reasoning process. To bridge this gap, we introduce FINEREASON, a logic-puzzle benchmark for fine-grained evaluation of LLMs' reasoning capabilities. Each puzzle can be decomposed into atomic steps, making it ideal for rigorous validation of intermediate correctness. Building on this, we introduce two tasks: state checking, and state transition, for a comprehensive evaluation of how models assess the current situation and plan the next move. To support broader research, we also provide a puzzle training set aimed at enhancing performance on general mathematical tasks. We show that models trained on our state checking and transition data demonstrate gains in math reasoning by up to 5.1% on GSM8K.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FINEREASON, a new benchmark for evaluating the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) using logic puzzles. Unlike existing benchmarks that primarily focus on final answer accuracy, FINEREASON emphasizes fine-grained evaluation of intermediate reasoning steps through two tasks: state checking (assessing the solvability of a given puzzle state) and state transition (determining the next valid move).  The paper demonstrates limitations in general-purpose and even reasoning-oriented LLMs on this benchmark and shows that training with puzzle data can improve performance on mathematical reasoning tasks, suggesting a transfer of reasoning abilities.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Fine-grained Evaluation:** The core strength of this paper lies in its focus on evaluating intermediate reasoning steps. This addresses a significant limitation of existing benchmarks, which often treat LLMs as black boxes, only assessing final answer correctness.  The breakdown into "state checking" and "state transition" provides a more detailed understanding of where LLMs succeed or fail during problem-solving.
*   **Novel Benchmark:**  FINEREASON offers a novel approach to benchmark creation by focusing on logic puzzles and decomposing them into atomic actions, state checking and state transition. This provides a straightforward way to evaluate the internal reasoning process of LLMs, which helps mitigate issues of models superficially exploiting patterns in existing datasets.
*   **Task Design for Evaluation:** The creation of "state checking" and "state transition" tasks helps evaluate both retrospective evaluation and prospective analysis capabilities. These tasks are designed well for the analysis of reflection, correction, and exploration of alternative paths, thereby addressing limitations in the previous benchmarks.
*   **Training Data and Performance Improvement:** The study's results indicate that pre-training the models on state checking and transition data consistently boosts performance on reasoning, with an improvement of 5.1% on GSM8K.
*   **Clearly defined methodology:** The authors clearly explained the methodology for puzzle decomposition and constructing state checking and transition tasks.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Puzzle Types:** While FINEREASON covers several puzzle types, the scope remains limited. Expanding to more diverse puzzle genres (e.g., spatial reasoning puzzles, cryptic crosswords) could further broaden the benchmark's applicability. However, the authors have specified this as a direction for future extensions in the work.
*   **Textual Table Representation:**  The paper acknowledges using textual tables as the representation for puzzle states, which might limit the potential for evaluating multi-modal reasoning. A more versatile approach might be using co-ordinates, which the authors mention as a future research direction.
*   **Zero-Shot CoT Prompting:** Using Zero-shot CoT ensures the focus is on model's inherent reasoning abilities without excessive reliance on external assistance. While using advanced prompting techniques might enhance performance, it can shift the focus away from the evaluation of models' genuine reasoning abilities.
*   **Limited Scope of Reasoning Ability Transfer:** The paper demonstrates that puzzle-based data for training enhances performance in broader math problems. While significant, this leaves the scope for more reasoning ability transfer.

**Significance:**

The paper offers a valuable contribution to the LLM evaluation landscape.  It moves beyond simple accuracy metrics, providing a more nuanced assessment of reasoning abilities.  The proposed methodology and benchmark can facilitate the development of more robust and reliable reasoning models. The potential to improve general mathematical reasoning through puzzle-based training is also a significant finding. It highlights the fact that solving puzzles can augment LLM's ability to solve broad reasoning tasks, which can lead to better models in the future.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-designed benchmark and evaluation methodology that addresses a crucial gap in LLM assessment. While the scope of puzzle types and the representation format have limitations, the core idea of evaluating intermediate reasoning steps is highly valuable. The paper's findings on improving mathematical reasoning through puzzle-based training further enhance its significance. Therefore, the paper deserves a score that reflects its novelty and significance.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Beyond Natural Language Perplexity: Detecting Dead Code Poisoning in Code Generation Datasets
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20246v1)
- **Authors**: Chichien Tsai, Chiamu Yu, Yingdar Lin, Yusung Wu, Weibin Lee
- **Abstract**: The increasing adoption of large language models (LLMs) for code-related tasks has raised concerns about the security of their training datasets. One critical threat is dead code poisoning, where syntactically valid but functionally redundant code is injected into training data to manipulate model behavior. Such attacks can degrade the performance of neural code search systems, leading to biased or insecure code suggestions. Existing detection methods, such as token-level perplexity analysis, fail to effectively identify dead code due to the structural and contextual characteristics of programming languages. In this paper, we propose DePA (Dead Code Perplexity Analysis), a novel line-level detection and cleansing method tailored to the structural properties of code. DePA computes line-level perplexity by leveraging the contextual relationships between code lines and identifies anomalous lines by comparing their perplexity to the overall distribution within the file. Our experiments on benchmark datasets demonstrate that DePA significantly outperforms existing methods, achieving 0.14-0.19 improvement in detection F1-score and a 44-65% increase in poisoned segment localization precision. Furthermore, DePA enhances detection speed by 0.62-23x, making it practical for large-scale dataset cleansing. Overall, by addressing the unique challenges of dead code poisoning, DePA provides a robust and efficient solution for safeguarding the integrity of code generation model training datasets.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Beyond Natural Language Perplexity: Detecting Dead Code Poisoning in Code Generation Datasets" addresses the problem of dead code poisoning in training datasets used for code generation models. It proposes a novel method called DEPA (Dead Code Perplexity Analysis) which detects poisoned code by analyzing line-level perplexity, considering the contextual relationships between code lines within a file.  DEPA computes the perplexity of each line in the context of the other lines in the code snippet, and then compares that perplexity to the distribution of perplexities across all lines in the file. The authors demonstrate that DEPA outperforms existing token-level perplexity methods in terms of detection F1-score, poisoned segment localization precision, detection speed, and AUROC. They evaluate DEPA on multiple benchmark datasets and also examine its resilience against adaptive attacks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addressing an Important Problem:**  The paper tackles a critical security concern in the increasingly important domain of code generation models.  Dead code poisoning is a real threat that can significantly compromise the reliability and security of these models.
    *   **Novelty of Approach:** DEPA offers a genuinely novel approach by shifting the focus from token-level perplexity (used in natural language poisoning detection) to line-level perplexity analysis tailored for code. The contextual aspect of code is well-captured by comparing perplexity of a line given all the other lines in the snippets vs. just considering its own tokens.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents compelling empirical evidence demonstrating the superiority of DEPA over baseline methods across several metrics and datasets. The gains in F1-score, localization precision, and speed are significant and well-supported. The adaptive attack analysis, while showing some vulnerability, also indicates a degree of robustness.
    *   **Efficiency:** The method is reported to be efficient in terms of detection speed which is a key factor for pre-processing large training datasets.
    *   **Well Written and Organized:** The paper is clearly written, well-organized, and easy to follow.  The method is explained in detail, and the experiments are well-designed and documented.
    *   **Code Available:** The inclusion of code on Github enhances the accessibility and reproducibility of the research, allowing others to build upon and validate the findings.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Language Scope:** The method is primarily designed for and evaluated on Python code. The authors acknowledge that adaptation to other languages (e.g., C++) might require adjustments due to language-specific syntax and coding conventions. This limits the generalizability of the approach.
    *   **Perplexity Can Be Circumvented:** While the adaptive attack analysis provides some insight into DEPA's robustness, it's conceivable that more sophisticated poisoning strategies could be devised to evade the detection mechanism. Perplexity, while a good indicator, isn't a perfect measure of anomalousness.  The attacker has a clear objective function that is easy to optimize, that is, reduce the perplexity score of poisoned code.
    *   **Reliance on a Code LLM:** The method's performance is tied to the quality and effectiveness of the underlying code LLM (CodeLlama in this case). The authors highlight this dependency in their analysis.
    *   **False Positives and Complex Code:**  The paper notes DEPA can produce false positives with highly covert techniques that tamper with variable or function names. It's also reasonable to expect that DEPA may struggle with accurate detection in very complex or unconventional code structures.
    *   **No Comparison to other code specific methods:** The paper compares its approach to ONION, which is originally not designed for code. It would be interesting to compare with other methods targeting code-level vulnerabilities.
    *   **Limited Discussion on Practical Deployment**: How does DEPA fit in a real world workflow, considering the need for maintenance? Are the parameters easily tuneable for different code styles/environments?

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper makes a significant contribution by addressing a relevant security concern.
    *   The proposed method, DEPA, offers a practical and effective approach for detecting dead code poisoning in code generation datasets.
    *   The empirical results and analysis provide valuable insights into the strengths and limitations of perplexity-based methods for this task.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7** for this paper.

*   The paper addresses an important and timely problem with a novel and well-evaluated approach (points for relevance and novelty).
*   The significant performance improvements over existing methods are impressive and suggest that DEPA could have a real impact on improving the security of code generation models (points for empirical results and potential impact).
*   The limitations regarding language scope, vulnerability to advanced poisoning techniques, and dependence on the code LLM's performance prevent a higher score (points deducted for generalizability and security loopholes). A score above 7 would suggest the paper is practically ready to be used on an industrial level, which it is not due to the limitations.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLM as a Broken Telephone: Iterative Generation Distorts Information
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20258v1)
- **Authors**: Amr Mohamed, Mingmeng Geng, Michalis Vazirgiannis, Guokan Shang
- **Abstract**: As large language models are increasingly responsible for online content, concerns arise about the impact of repeatedly processing their own outputs. Inspired by the "broken telephone" effect in chained human communication, this study investigates whether LLMs similarly distort information through iterative generation. Through translation-based experiments, we find that distortion accumulates over time, influenced by language choice and chain complexity. While degradation is inevitable, it can be mitigated through strategic prompting techniques. These findings contribute to discussions on the long-term effects of AI-mediated information propagation, raising important questions about the reliability of LLM-generated content in iterative workflows.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LLM as a Broken Telephone: Iterative Generation Distorts Information":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether Large Language Models (LLMs) distort information when iteratively processing their own outputs, similar to the "broken telephone" effect in human communication.  The authors conduct translation-based experiments where a document in English is translated into another language and back to English across multiple iterations using LLMs. They then compare the back-translated version to the original English text, measuring textual relevance and factuality preservation. The study finds that information distortion accumulates over time, influenced by language choice and chain complexity, but can be mitigated by strategic prompting techniques.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea – examining LLMs for the "broken telephone" effect – is conceptually interesting and relevant given the increasing reliance on LLMs to generate and process online content. While the concept of "model collapse" and information drift is known, *applying* this specifically to a chained *translation* and *iterative self-processing* setup offers a specific contribution that sets it apart from a general "memorization" or "regurgitation" issue. Moreover, the focus on *factuality* within the context of iterative LLM usage is relatively under-explored compared to aspects like toxicity or style drift, as noted by the paper itself.

*   **Significance:** The findings are significant because they highlight potential pitfalls of using LLMs in workflows that involve iterative refinement and reprocessing of generated content.  In the age where AI-generated content is being used to train future AI models, as well as shape the way we obtain information, there are serious considerations of its validity. If errors accumulate with each iteration, it could lead to a degradation of the quality of information present online.  The study also has implications for multi-agent systems where LLMs communicate and collaborate.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined experiments:** The experimental setups (bilingual self-loop, bilingual two-player, multilingual multiplayer) are clearly described and allow for systematic investigation of different aspects of the problem.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The use of multiple metrics for textual relevance and factuality preservation provides a holistic view of the distortion process.
    *   **Actionable insights:** The study identifies factors that influence distortion (language choice, chain complexity) and suggests mitigation strategies (temperature control, constrained prompting), which is valuable for practitioners.
    *   **Relevance:**  The use of relatively contemporary models (Llama and Mistral series) strengthens the practical relevance of the findings compared to experiments using models that are becoming less commonplace.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The study primarily focuses on translation tasks, which may not be representative of all iterative LLM workflows. Other generative tasks, such as summarization or content generation, could exhibit different patterns of distortion. The inclusion of a rephrasing ablation experiment partially addresses this, but could be expanded.
    *   **Factuality Evaluation:** The evaluation of factuality relies on the assumption that the original text is factually correct. While stated by the authors, this assumption should be acknowledged and addressed as this can create bias.  The judge LLM also possesses its own potential for bias, which needs to be noted.
    *   **Model Size Limited:** Using models with only 7-9 billion parameters is understandable from a resource constraint perspective, but may not fully generalize to much larger models. The larger model in Section 4.3 helped partially address this, but could still be more robust.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical Explanation:** While the paper identifies *what* happens, it lacks a deeper theoretical explanation *why* certain language pairings or chain configurations exacerbate distortion. A more in-depth analysis of the internal representations and biases of the LLMs could strengthen the paper.

*   **Overall Assessment:**

The paper offers valuable insights into the potential for information distortion in iterative LLM workflows, a growing concern as AI-generated content becomes more prevalent. While the scope is somewhat limited to translation, the well-designed experiments, comprehensive evaluation, and actionable insights make a solid contribution to the field.  The insights are practical, and it raises an important question about information fidelity in LLM-mediated systems. Further research is needed to extend these findings to other generative tasks, larger models, and to develop more robust mitigation strategies. While its methodology is sound and its findings are clearly communicated, it could benefit from a deeper dive into the theoretical underpinnings of *why* the distortion occurs.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**
The paper addresses a pertinent concern about information drift in AI. The methodical investigation and practical recommendations enhance its significance. It is not a groundbreaking theoretical leap forward (hence not scoring higher), but a well-executed and relevant empirical study with clear practical implications. The limitations identified above (scope, assumptions, lack of deeper theoretical explanation) prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Large Language Models as Attribution Regularizers for Efficient Model Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20268v1)
- **Authors**: Davor Vukadin, Marin Šilić, Goran Delač
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable performance across diverse domains. However, effectively leveraging their vast knowledge for training smaller downstream models remains an open challenge, especially in domains like tabular data learning, where simpler models are often preferred due to interpretability and efficiency. In this paper, we introduce a novel yet straightforward method for incorporating LLM-generated global task feature attributions into the training process of smaller networks. Specifically, we propose an attribution-matching regularization term that aligns the training dynamics of the smaller model with the insights provided by the LLM. By doing so, our approach yields superior performance in few-shot learning scenarios. Notably, our method requires only black-box API access to the LLM, making it easy to integrate into existing training pipelines with minimal computational overhead. Furthermore, we demonstrate how this method can be used to address common issues in real-world datasets, such as skewness and bias. By integrating high-level knowledge from LLMs, our approach improves generalization, even when training data is limited or imbalanced. We validate its effectiveness through extensive experiments across multiple tasks, demonstrating improved learning efficiency and model robustness.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LAAT (Large Language Model Attribution Aligned Training), a novel regularization method that leverages the knowledge embedded in Large Language Models (LLMs) to improve the training of smaller downstream models, particularly in few-shot learning scenarios and when dealing with biased data. LAAT uses LLMs to generate feature importance scores, which are then used as a regularization term during the training of smaller models, encouraging them to align their feature attributions with the insights provided by the LLM.  The method requires only black-box API access to the LLM, making it easily integrable with minimal computational overhead. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of LAAT through extensive experiments across multiple tabular datasets, showing improved performance in few-shot scenarios and increased robustness to data bias.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The idea of using LLMs as a source of knowledge for regularizing the training of smaller models isn't entirely new. Previous works have explored knowledge distillation and LLM-generated synthetic data for this purpose. However, LAAT distinguishes itself by using *attributions* generated by LLMs rather than direct predictions or data augmentation.  The novelty lies primarily in the specific approach of aligning feature-wise explanations with LLM-generated global explanations. Using input gradients aligned with LLM-derived feature importance scores is a relatively straightforward approach, however. FeatLLM, which constructs preprocessing pipelines to transform the original data into one suited for few-shot inference, seems to be far more intricate. The paper's claims of superior performance, however, do bolster the argument that the technique is effective.

**Significance:** Tabular data is a key focus for real-world applications, especially in domains where interpretability and efficiency are essential, and LLMs traditionally struggle in this domain. Improving the performance of smaller, more interpretable models through LLM guidance could have a real impact.  LAAT's potential to mitigate bias in datasets is also significant, as biased data is a common problem that can lead to unfair or inaccurate models. The practical appeal of LAAT is strengthened by its requirement for only black-box API access to the LLM, thus minimizing computational costs and integration complexity. Compared to full fine-tuning of LLMs, this is a significant advantage. The paper shows substantial performance gains over multiple benchmark datasets with the technique and presents a basic hyperparameter study for the technique, furthering practical applicability.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear and well-defined method:** The LAAT method is easy to understand and implement.
*   **Strong experimental results:** The paper provides comprehensive experimental results on several datasets, demonstrating consistent improvements over baseline methods.
*   **Practical advantages:** The reliance on black-box LLM access makes the method computationally efficient and easy to integrate into existing pipelines.
*   **Bias mitigation:** The demonstration of LAAT's ability to mitigate bias in datasets is a valuable contribution.
*   **Addresses an important problem:** Improving few-shot learning and bias robustness in tabular data modeling is a relevant and impactful problem.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited novelty:** The core idea of using LLMs for knowledge transfer is not entirely new, and the specific attribution alignment approach, while effective, isn't particularly complex.
*   **Simplicity:** The method seems somewhat simplistic. It is unclear whether the LLM derived feature importance scores represent the upper bound of performance.
*   **LLM dependency:** The method's performance is heavily dependent on the quality of the LLM and its ability to provide accurate feature importance scores.  The selection of Llama 3.3, Gemini 2.0 Flash, and GPT-40-mini also introduces further dependency that may be hard to test.
*   **Hyperparameter tuning:** While the paper includes a basic hyperparameter study, further investigation into the sensitivity of LAAT to different hyperparameter configurations would be beneficial, as would a more rigorous ablation study that would further cement the impact of the LAAT method.

**Potential Influence:**

The simplicity and effectiveness of LAAT could make it a popular technique for practitioners working with tabular data. The method's ability to improve few-shot learning and mitigate bias could encourage further research in this area. Further examination on a variety of applications and more complex scenarios could solidify the use of LAAT in diverse applications.

**Justification for the score:**

While the experimental results are compelling and the method is relatively simple and practical, the limited novelty and strong dependence on the LLM's performance prevent a higher score. The work fills a notable gap between large and small models and improves upon existing approaches, but doesn't fundamentally change the landscape. The simplicity of the approach, while a strength in terms of usability, also means that the contribution is incremental rather than revolutionary. Furthermore, the limited hyperparameter study limits practical use.

### Explainable, Multi-modal Wound Infection Classification from Images Augmented with Generated Captions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20277v1)
- **Authors**: Palawat Busaranuvong, Emmanuel Agu, Reza Saadati Fard, Deepak Kumar, Shefalika Gautam, Bengisu Tulu, Diane Strong
- **Abstract**: Infections in Diabetic Foot Ulcers (DFUs) can cause severe complications, including tissue death and limb amputation, highlighting the need for accurate, timely diagnosis. Previous machine learning methods have focused on identifying infections by analyzing wound images alone, without utilizing additional metadata such as medical notes. In this study, we aim to improve infection detection by introducing Synthetic Caption Augmented Retrieval for Wound Infection Detection (SCARWID), a novel deep learning framework that leverages synthetic textual descriptions to augment DFU images. SCARWID consists of two components: (1) Wound-BLIP, a Vision-Language Model (VLM) fine-tuned on GPT-4o-generated descriptions to synthesize consistent captions from images; and (2) an Image-Text Fusion module that uses cross-attention to extract cross-modal embeddings from an image and its corresponding Wound-BLIP caption. Infection status is determined by retrieving the top-k similar items from a labeled support set. To enhance the diversity of training data, we utilized a latent diffusion model to generate additional wound images. As a result, SCARWID outperformed state-of-the-art models, achieving average sensitivity, specificity, and accuracy of 0.85, 0.78, and 0.81, respectively, for wound infection classification. Displaying the generated captions alongside the wound images and infection detection results enhances interpretability and trust, enabling nurses to align SCARWID outputs with their medical knowledge. This is particularly valuable when wound notes are unavailable or when assisting novice nurses who may find it difficult to identify visual attributes of wound infection.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SCARWID (Synthetic Caption Augmented Retrieval for Wound Infection Detection), a novel deep learning framework designed to improve the accuracy and interpretability of infection detection in Diabetic Foot Ulcers (DFUs). SCARWID utilizes synthetic textual descriptions generated from images using a fine-tuned Vision-Language Model (Wound-BLIP).  The framework combines image features with textual descriptions through an Image-Text Fusion module and classifies infection status by retrieving similar items from a labeled support set.  The authors also use a latent diffusion model to generate additional wound images for data augmentation. The results demonstrate that SCARWID outperforms existing state-of-the-art models in sensitivity, specificity, and accuracy for wound infection classification, while also enhancing interpretability via visual highlights, annotations, and corresponding wound descriptions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the integrated approach of using synthetic captions generated from a vision-language model (Wound-BLIP) to augment DFU images for infection detection. The use of textual metadata to enhance image-based classification, and the emphasis on generating that metadata synthetically when unavailable in real-world scenarios, is a key contribution. The use of a similarity-based retrieval method, combined with data augmentation using a latent diffusion model, also adds to the novelty, although these elements have seen some prior usage in isolation. The explainability aspect using Grad-CAM and attention maps enhances the utility for medical professionals.
*   **Significance:** Improving the accuracy and speed of DFU infection detection is clinically significant, potentially reducing amputations and healthcare costs. The interpretability aspect is also valuable, fostering trust and aiding in the training of novice nurses. The paper shows a clear performance improvement over existing state-of-the-art methods on the Part-B DFU dataset. The relatively small size of the dataset, and the lack of real-world deployment or validation with clinical practitioners are limitations.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The integrated framework combining vision and language models demonstrates superior performance compared to image-only methods.
    *   The focus on interpretability makes the system more useful for clinical decision-making.
    *   Data augmentation techniques further improve performance.
    *   The use of similarity-based classification offers a different paradigm from traditional probability-based methods.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The study relies on a publicly available dataset, which might not fully represent the variability of real-world DFU images.
    *   The synthetic data generation, while enhancing performance, adds complexity to the model and raises concerns about the potential for bias.  Hallucinations in the text generated by the Wound-BLIP remain a concern.
    *   The improvement, while significant, is incremental and relies on several components each making a small contribution.
    *   The paper lacks validation in a real-world clinical setting with medical practitioners. There is no actual assessment of how the system affects clinical workflows or patient outcomes.
    * The contribution of the similarity-based classification is not discussed in depth

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in medical image analysis by demonstrating the benefits of incorporating textual metadata and explainability techniques. The methods introduced could be adapted for other medical imaging tasks where interpretability is crucial. The RAG is noted as a valuable next step, this is in part due to concerns surrounding the use of MLLMs and the variable dataset quality.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel and significant contribution. It combines existing techniques in a creative way to address a clinically relevant problem. The performance improvements are statistically significant and the explainability aspect is a clear positive. However, several factors limit its overall impact. The study lacks real-world validation, relies on synthetic data generation which introduces potential bias, and only shows incremental improvements. As a result, it makes a contribution, but falls short of presenting transformative results. Therefore,

### Evaluating Human Trust in LLM-Based Planners: A Preliminary Study
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20284v1)
- **Authors**: Shenghui Chen, Yunhao Yang, Kayla Boggess, Seongkook Heo, Lu Feng, Ufuk Topcu
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used for planning tasks, offering unique capabilities not found in classical planners such as generating explanations and iterative refinement. However, trust--a critical factor in the adoption of planning systems--remains underexplored in the context of LLM-based planning tasks. This study bridges this gap by comparing human trust in LLM-based planners with classical planners through a user study in a Planning Domain Definition Language (PDDL) domain. Combining subjective measures, such as trust questionnaires, with objective metrics like evaluation accuracy, our findings reveal that correctness is the primary driver of trust and performance. Explanations provided by the LLM improved evaluation accuracy but had limited impact on trust, while plan refinement showed potential for increasing trust without significantly enhancing evaluation accuracy.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper, including a novelty/significance score:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates human trust in LLM-based planners compared to classical PDDL solvers in a planning domain.  It conducts a user study examining the effects of plan correctness, explanations, and refinement on user trust and evaluation accuracy. The findings suggest that plan correctness is the primary driver of trust and performance.  Explanations improved evaluation accuracy but had little impact on trust, while plan refinement showed potential for increasing trust without significantly improving evaluation accuracy, raising concerns about potential overtrust.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses an important gap:** The paper directly tackles the under-explored area of human trust in LLM-based planners, a crucial factor for real-world adoption.  Most existing work focuses primarily on technical performance.
    *   **Well-designed user study:** The use of a within-subjects design, counterbalancing, and inclusion of both subjective (trust questionnaires) and objective (evaluation accuracy) measures strengthens the experimental methodology. The use of the PDDL domain provides a clear, measurable environment.
    *   **Interesting findings:** The core finding that correctness is the dominant factor is valuable, and the nuanced results regarding explanations and refinement provide actionable insights. The observation about LLM refinement potentially leading to overtrust is particularly important.
    *   **Practical implications:** The discussion of human-centered AI planning systems based on the study's findings is a welcome contribution.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty in the core methods:**  The evaluation methods (user studies, questionnaires) and trust metrics are relatively standard in the human-computer interaction and AI trust literature. The novelty lies primarily in *applying* these techniques to the *specific context* of LLM-based planning.
    *   **Modest effect sizes and Statistical Significance:** Some of the findings, while conceptually important, exhibit only modest effect sizes, and some comparisons (e.g., comparing the LLM planner to PDDL solver on evaluation accuracy) lack statistical significance, which could result from a small sample size.
    *   **Simplistic planning domain:** The Gripper domain, while well-defined, is relatively simple compared to real-world planning scenarios. This limits the generalizability of the results. Trust dynamics may differ significantly with more complex, safety-critical, or high-stakes planning tasks.
    *   **Limited exploration of explanation content:** The manual explanations used in the study may not be representative of LLM-generated explanations. A more rigorous content analysis or generation of explanations by the LLM itself could have yielded more insights.
    *   **Lack of specific analysis regarding the reason for mistrust:** it remains unclear why refinement increased trust; there might be psychological factors at play that were not accounted for in the study design.
    *  **LLM capabilities have progressed:** given that the paper was submitted on Feb 27, 2025 using LLM Planner(GPT-40 [1]), it is not entirely clear that current planning capabilities of larger language models such as GPT-4 are fairly represented in the study. It would be interesting to see an extension of the paper performed with much more capable language models.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper provides a valuable initial empirical contribution to the nascent field of human trust in LLM-based planning.
    *   The findings can inform the design of more trustworthy and effective LLM planners, potentially leading to greater user acceptance and safer deployment.
    *   The study highlights the importance of considering psychological factors (e.g., overtrust) when evaluating and deploying LLMs in planning applications.
    *   The study sets a foundation for future research, providing a methodological template and raising critical questions for further investigation.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper addresses an important and timely issue, its reliance on standard methodologies and the limitations of its planning domain temper its overall novelty and significance. The study's empirical contribution and practical implications are valuable, but the modest effect sizes and potential for limited generalizability prevent it from being considered a truly exceptional contribution. Further research is needed to confirm and extend the findings in more complex and realistic settings.

### Conformal Tail Risk Control for Large Language Model Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20285v1)
- **Authors**: Catherine Yu-Chi Chen, Jingyan Shen, Zhun Deng, Lihua Lei
- **Abstract**: Recent developments in large language models (LLMs) have led to their widespread usage for various tasks. The prevalence of LLMs in society implores the assurance on the reliability of their performance. In particular, risk-sensitive applications demand meticulous attention to unexpectedly poor outcomes, i.e., tail events, for instance, toxic answers, humiliating language, and offensive outputs. Due to the costly nature of acquiring human annotations, general-purpose scoring models have been created to automate the process of quantifying these tail events. This phenomenon introduces potential human-machine misalignment between the respective scoring mechanisms. In this work, we present a lightweight calibration framework for blackbox models that ensures the alignment of humans and machines with provable guarantees. Our framework provides a rigorous approach to controlling any distortion risk measure that is characterized by a weighted average of quantiles of the loss incurred by the LLM with high confidence. The theoretical foundation of our method relies on the connection between conformal risk control and a traditional family of statistics, i.e., L-statistics. To demonstrate the utility of our framework, we conduct comprehensive experiments that address the issue of human-machine misalignment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Conformal Tail Risk Control for Large Language Model Alignment":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of human-machine misalignment in Large Language Models (LLMs) when using machine-generated scores (e.g., toxicity scores from Detoxify) to control for undesirable outputs (e.g., toxic responses).  It proposes a lightweight calibration framework based on conformal risk control, ensuring alignment between human and machine assessments with provable guarantees. The core idea is to control distortion risk measures, defined as weighted averages of quantiles of the human-rated disutility, at a pre-specified level. The framework leverages L-statistics to derive finite-sample risk control bounds and avoids computationally expensive model refitting. Experimental results demonstrate the method's effectiveness in mitigating toxicity while being less conservative than alternative conformal risk control methods (DKW and BJ statistics) and adapting to varying degrees of human-machine alignment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Problem Relevance:** The paper tackles a very important and practical problem in LLM deployment – the misalignment between automatically generated scores and true human perception of risk (e.g., toxicity, offensiveness).  This misalignment can lead to ineffective risk mitigation and unexpected negative consequences.
*   **Sound Methodology:** The use of conformal risk control and L-statistics is a statistically rigorous approach to addressing the problem. The choice of controlling *distortion* risk measures is well-justified as it allows for focusing on tail events (rare, but high-impact undesirable outputs). The approach offers finite-sample guarantees, a crucial aspect for ensuring reliability in risk-sensitive applications.
*   **Lightweight and Practical:** Unlike methods such as RLHF, the proposed calibration framework is computationally efficient because it does *not* require model refitting. It essentially involves tuning a single parameter, making it more practical to deploy in real-world settings. This is a significant advantage.
*   **Clear Theoretical Foundation:** The paper presents a solid theoretical foundation, building upon existing work on conformal risk control and extending it to handle distortion risk measures via the lens of L-statistics. The theorems and proofs (in the appendix) demonstrate the technical rigor of the approach.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments, even with semi-synthetic data, provide evidence that the proposed method (CDRC-L) performs well compared to DKW and BJ statistics variants within the conformal risk control framework. The experiments also show adaptability to human-machine misalignment and effectiveness in reducing deployment cost (measured by average sampling cost).

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Semi-Synthetic Data:** The primary weakness lies in the reliance on a semi-synthetic benchmark. Using Detoxify as a proxy for "human" annotation simplifies the problem and may not fully capture the complexities and nuances of real human judgment.  While the paper acknowledges this limitation and varies the degree of misalignment, validating the approach with *actual* human annotation data would significantly strengthen the results.
*   **Limited Scope of Risk Measures:** While the focus on distortion risk measures is justified, the experiments primarily concentrate on CVaR and VaR. Exploring other relevant risk measures and demonstrating the method's generalizability across a broader set of risk metrics could further enhance the paper's impact.
*   **Black-Box Nature:** Although the method is lightweight and non-parametric, the reliance on a black-box LLM and machine scorer might limit explainability and interpretability.
*   **Assumptions:** While the paper claims no assumptions about the underlying LLM, there is an assumption that the data points are i.i.d. Acknowledging this assumption and investigating potential extensions to address non-i.i.d. settings (e.g. distribution shifts in prompt populations) would improve the robustness of the method.
*   **Practical deployment costs:** While the framework attempts to mitigate risks while maintaining cost efficiency, it still relies on re-generating responses to reduce potential for misaligned LLM-generated answers, this could result in high computational and energy costs depending on size and complexity of the task, therefore it would be useful for further work to investigate a wider range of deployment strategies to minimise this.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's novelty lies in its adaptation of conformal risk control to address the specific challenges of LLM alignment, particularly the misalignment between machine-generated scores and human perception of risk. The extension to distortion risk measures and the use of L-statistics provide a theoretical framework for controlling tail risks in a distribution-free manner. The computational efficiency and practical nature of the proposed method (compared to RLHF or full model retraining) contribute to its significance. It provides a theoretically sound and relatively easy-to-implement approach for mitigating undesirable LLM outputs in risk-sensitive applications.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by providing a practical and theoretically sound method for addressing human-machine misalignment in LLMs. The use of conformal risk control and L-statistics is novel in this context, and the emphasis on controlling tail risks is highly relevant. However, the reliance on semi-synthetic data and the limited scope of risk measures prevent the paper from achieving a higher score.  If the authors were to validate the approach with real human annotations and broaden the scope of risk metrics explored, the impact of the paper would be significantly enhanced. Despite its weaknesses, this work offers a significant step toward building more reliable and trustworthy LLM systems.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Judge a Book by its Cover: Investigating Multi-Modal LLMs for Multi-Page Handwritten Document Transcription
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20295v1)
- **Authors**: Benjamin Gutteridge, Matthew Thomas Jackson, Toni Kukurin, Xiaowen Dong
- **Abstract**: Handwritten text recognition (HTR) remains a challenging task, particularly for multi-page documents where pages share common formatting and contextual features. While modern optical character recognition (OCR) engines are proficient with printed text, their performance on handwriting is limited, often requiring costly labeled data for fine-tuning. In this paper, we explore the use of multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) for transcribing multi-page handwritten documents in a zero-shot setting. We investigate various configurations of commercial OCR engines and MLLMs, utilizing the latter both as end-to-end transcribers and as post-processors, with and without image components. We propose a novel method, '+first page', which enhances MLLM transcription by providing the OCR output of the entire document along with just the first page image. This approach leverages shared document features without incurring the high cost of processing all images. Experiments on a multi-page version of the IAM Handwriting Database demonstrate that '+first page' improves transcription accuracy, balances cost with performance, and even enhances results on out-of-sample text by extrapolating formatting and OCR error patterns from a single page.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the use of Multi-Modal Large Language Models (MLLMs) for transcribing multi-page handwritten documents in a zero-shot setting. It compares various configurations of commercial OCR engines and MLLMs, using the latter both as end-to-end transcribers and as post-processors, with and without image components. The key contribution is a novel method called "+FIRST PAGE," where the MLLM is provided with OCR output of the entire document along with just the first page image. This leverages shared document features without the high cost of processing all images. Experiments on a modified IAM Handwriting Database show that "+FIRST PAGE" improves transcription accuracy, balances cost with performance, and even enhances results on out-of-sample text by extrapolating formatting and OCR error patterns from a single page.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Problem:** The paper tackles a significant and practical problem: efficiently transcribing multi-page handwritten documents. This is a common challenge in many industries and research fields where historical records or hand-filled forms exist.
    *   **Zero-Shot Approach:**  The focus on a zero-shot setting is valuable. Fine-tuning for handwriting recognition is data-intensive, and a zero-shot method significantly reduces the barrier to entry for real-world applications.
    *   **"+FIRST PAGE" Novelty:**  The "+FIRST PAGE" method is a novel and clever way to leverage the contextual information within a document and the pattern recognition abilities of MLLMs without incurring the high cost of processing every page image.
    *   **Experimental Rigor:** The experiments are well-designed and use a modified version of a standard benchmark dataset (IAM). The comparison of various OCR engines, MLLMs, and prompting strategies provides a comprehensive analysis. The analysis of cost vs. performance is a valuable addition.
    *   **Interesting findings regarding PBP approach**: The authors identify a performance degradation for all-at-once methods as document length increases, even within the context window limit, and attribute this to potentially the 'Lost in the Middle' effect, providing directions for future research.
    *   **Out-of-sample extrapolation** The experiments clearly demonstrate that MLLMs can effectively extrapolate formatting and error correction from a single "first page" to other pages in the document.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Commercial MLLMs:** The paper relies heavily on commercial MLLMs (GPT-40 and GPT-40-MINI) accessed through the OpenAI API. This makes the results less reproducible for researchers without access to these APIs, and the performance is subject to change as OpenAI updates its models.  While using commercial models is practical, a comparison with open-source MLLMs, if possible, would strengthen the contribution.
    *   **Limited Dataset Modification:** The creation of the multi-page dataset by randomly combining pages by writer ID might not fully capture the complexities of real-world multi-page documents, where content across pages is often related. A dataset with connected content across pages would be a more challenging and relevant evaluation.
    *   **Limited Scope on Error Correction:** While the paper mentions "error catching," the description of the error correction mechanism is quite high-level. A more detailed explanation, perhaps with specific examples, of how catastrophic MLLM errors are identified and mitigated would be beneficial.
    *   **Why GPT-40 MINI worked better**: While the authors did speculate on why GPT-40 MINI worked better, it would strengthen the paper if they provided specific examples of how GPT-40 was 'overdoing it', or if they compared the vision components of the two GPT models.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The "+FIRST PAGE" method provides a practical and cost-effective approach to improving the transcription accuracy of multi-page handwritten documents using MLLMs.  This is a step forward in making these powerful models accessible for real-world applications.
    *   The paper highlights the potential of MLLMs as not just post-processors for OCR but also as end-to-end transcribers, especially with the vision* PBP method showing surprisingly powerful results without explicit OCR input.
    *   The insights into long-context performance limitations and the impact of page breaks offer valuable directions for future research on improving MLLMs for document processing.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and practical method for a significant problem, with solid experimental support. The "+FIRST PAGE" approach and the insights into cost-performance trade-offs are valuable contributions. However, the reliance on commercial APIs limits reproducibility, and the dataset modification and error correction mechanisms could be explored in more depth. Furthermore, the paper lacks comparison to open-source models and analysis of each model's individual strengths and weaknesses.

Overall, the paper offers a meaningful contribution to the field of document understanding and provides a promising direction for future research on leveraging MLLMs for handwritten document transcription.

### An exploration of features to improve the generalisability of fake news detection models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.20299v1)
- **Authors**: Nathaniel Hoy, Theodora Koulouri
- **Abstract**: Fake news poses global risks by influencing elections and spreading misinformation, making detection critical. Existing NLP and supervised Machine Learning methods perform well under cross-validation but struggle to generalise across datasets, even within the same domain. This issue stems from coarsely labelled training data, where articles are labelled based on their publisher, introducing biases that token-based models like TF-IDF and BERT are sensitive to. While Large Language Models (LLMs) offer promise, their application in fake news detection remains limited. This study demonstrates that meaningful features can still be extracted from coarsely labelled data to improve real-world robustness. Stylistic features-lexical, syntactic, and semantic-are explored due to their reduced sensitivity to dataset biases. Additionally, novel social-monetisation features are introduced, capturing economic incentives behind fake news, such as advertisements, external links, and social media elements. The study trains on the coarsely labelled NELA 2020-21 dataset and evaluates using the manually labelled Facebook URLs dataset, a gold standard for generalisability. Results highlight the limitations of token-based models trained on biased data and contribute to the scarce evidence on LLMs like LLaMa in this field. Findings indicate that stylistic and social-monetisation features offer more generalisable predictions than token-based methods and LLMs. Statistical and permutation feature importance analyses further reveal their potential to enhance performance and mitigate dataset biases, providing a path forward for improving fake news detection.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper addresses the problem of poor generalizability in fake news detection models, a common issue when models trained on one dataset perform poorly on others. The authors argue that this problem is exacerbated by the use of coarsely labeled datasets and reliance on token-based representations (e.g., TF-IDF, BERT). They propose using stylistic features (linguistic, psycholinguistic) and introduce novel "social-monetisation" features (frequency of ads, external links, social media share links) as a more generalizable approach. They train models on the NELA 2020-21 dataset and evaluate them using an external, manually labeled Facebook URLs dataset.  The results indicate that models using stylistic and social-monetisation features exhibit better generalizability and balanced performance compared to token-based models, including LLaMa.  The paper also identifies a reduced feature set that maintains performance while improving computational efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses an Important Problem:** The paper tackles a key challenge in fake news detection: the lack of generalizability. Many existing studies focus on achieving high accuracy on specific datasets without considering how well the models perform in real-world scenarios or on unseen data.
*   **Novel Feature Set:** The introduction of "social-monetisation" features is a significant contribution. These features capture economic incentives that drive the creation and spread of fake news, a factor often overlooked in previous studies.
*   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The use of an external, manually labeled Facebook URLs dataset for evaluation is a major strength. This dataset provides a more realistic and reliable assessment of generalizability compared to cross-validation on coarsely labeled datasets. The paper clearly delineates the evaluation methodology.
*   **Balanced Performance:** The finding that stylistic features and social monetisation features demonstrate more balanced recall and specificity than token-based features is an interesting insight. This shows that the proposed features are less prone to misclassification.
*   **Actionable Findings:** The reduced feature set identified through permutation feature importance offers practical guidance for building efficient and effective fake news detection models.
* **Comparative Evaluation of LLMs:** The paper includes a comparative analysis of LLaMa across different configurations (zero-shot, few-shot, and fine-tuned), providing valuable insights into the capabilities and limitations of LLMs in the context of fake news detection.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Dataset Diversity:** While the use of the Facebook URLs dataset is a strength, the paper could benefit from evaluation on a broader range of external datasets, covering different types of fake news and different demographic groups.
*   **Model Simplicity:** The machine learning algorithms used (Logistic Regression, Random Forest, Gradient Boosting) are relatively simple. While this helps focus on feature engineering, it might limit the potential performance of the proposed features. Deeper and more complex models could be used to boost the performance.
